We see that normal batteries have low energy density in both cases, while conventional fuels have much higher energy density, especially per unit volume. On the other hand hydrogen has very low energy density per unit volume, however this can be increased by condensing it (liquefying it). 

Many factors need to be considered in the choice of an energy source: 

- Volumetric energy density 

- Specific energy density 

- Emissions 

- Policy 

- Compatibility 

- Safety 

- Infrastructure 

- Availability 

- Cost 

The only feasible alternative (to traditional fuels) energy sources for civil aviation in modern times can be classified into three classes: 

- **Drop-in fuels** : this contains sustainable alternatives such as biofuels and synthetic kerosene. They are compatible with current aero engines; 

- **Batteries** : electric powered engines are still only a concept but are being developed. The main disadvantage is the low specific and volumetric energy density of batteries. 

- **Cryogenic** : alternatives with low volumetric energy densities can still be used in aviation in liquid form. 

1 

The energy provided by the fuel is then turned into useable power. In aviation this is done by the gas turbine. 

Depending on the mission requirements there are different types of engine with varying power outputs: 

**==> picture [348 x 211] intentionally omitted <==**

For aero engines, this power needs to be then converted into thrust. This can be done in two ways: 

- **Nozzle** : the exhaust gasses pass through a convergent channel and their speed is greatly increased. 

- **Propeller** : the power produced is used to spin a propeller which generates thrust. Typically characterized by very high propulsive efficiency but only works for low Mach numbers. 

## **Thermodynamics summary** 

To fully understand how an aero engine works it is important to recap some basic thermodynamic concepts. 

There are 4 fundamental laws in thermodynamics, which state: 

**==> picture [301 x 97] intentionally omitted <==**

The first law states the conservation of energy in a closed system, in other words energy is converted from form to another, however the total value is conserved. This can be expressed as: 

**==> picture [113 x 21] intentionally omitted <==**

Where E is the total energy, U is the internal energy (which is a function of temperature), KE is the kinetic energy in the system and PE is the potential energy. The **internal energy** is a state variable, which means that it’s value depends on the first and last thermodynamic states of the system and not on the transformation. It can be expressed as: 

**==> picture [58 x 15] intentionally omitted <==**

2 

Another important thermodynamic quantity is **enthalpy** , which can be defined as: 

**==> picture [51 x 17] intentionally omitted <==**

Just like the internal energy it can be expressed as: 

**==> picture [58 x 20] intentionally omitted <==**

The second law introduces an additional quantity called **entropy** , which can be thought of as a measure of the disorder of a system. Just like enthalpy and internal energy, entropy is also a state variable so its value only depends on the initial and final thermodynamic states of a transformation. Specifically the second law states that the entropy of the universe always increases in a spontaneous (natural) process and remains unchanged in an equilibrium process (an ideal process in which all thermodynamic states are equilibrium states). 

In other words the second law defines the concept of irreversible processes, or processes during which the entropy of the system increases. These processes cannot be reversed and are caused by dissipation and losses. 

Entropy can be evaluated as: 

**==> picture [71 x 21] intentionally omitted <==**

Going back to the engine, let’s now look at the thermodynamics of a heat engine. A **heat engine** is a machine that operates between two heat reservoirs and completes a thermodynamic cycle. A generator is a heat engine which takes heat from the hot source, produces work and releases heat into the cold source. 

**==> picture [179 x 181] intentionally omitted <==**

Thermodynamic cycles are made up of different thermodynamic processes. These are usually **polytropic processes** , in other words processes which can be defined by the following relation: 

**==> picture [58 x 18] intentionally omitted <==**

Where n depends on the process: 

- n = 0: **isobaric process** 

- n = 1: **isothermal process** 

- n = k: **isentropic process** (where k is the specific heat capacity coefficient ratio) 

- • n = ∞: **isocoric** 

3 

**==> picture [376 x 163] intentionally omitted <==**

A gas turbine is nothing more than a heat engine in which a fluid (air) completes a thermodynamic cylce. This cycle is the **Joule-Brayton cycle** which is ideally made up of: 

- **Isentropic compression** : this occurs in the inlet and the compressor. In reality the process is adiabatic since there is an associated entropy increase due to losses. Work is added to the cycle with no heat involved. 

- **Isobaric combustion** : this occurs in the combustor. In reality it is not perfectly isobaric since there are pressure losses. Heat is added to the cycle with no work involved. 

- **Isentropic expansion** : this occurs in the turbines and the nozzle. In reality the process is adiabatic since there is an associated entropy increase due to losses. Work is extracted from the cycle with no heat involved. Part of the work extracted is used to run the compressors, while the remaining part is used to generate thrust. 

- **Isobaric refrigeration** : this is the last process required to close out the cycle, however it is not executed by the aero engine. It represents what occurs in the atmosphere: the hot exhaust gasses release heat into the atmosphere at constant atmospheric pressure. 

**==> picture [365 x 153] intentionally omitted <==**

**==> picture [229 x 181] intentionally omitted <==**

4 

Another example of a thermodynamic cycle is the **Rankine cycle** , which is made up of: 

- **Isentropic compression** : this occurs in the liquid phase 

- **Isobaric heating** : heat is added into the system. The fluid transitions and becomes vapor at a constant temperature and pressure. Once all of the vapor has transition more heat can be added. This last phase is called superheating (4-5). 

- **Isentropic expansion** : work is extracted by letting the vapor expand in a turbine 

- **Condensation** : heat is extracted from the system. All of the vapor transitions back to its liquid form. 

**==> picture [215 x 183] intentionally omitted <==**

Some power plants used these two cycles combined to obtain very high thermodynamic efficiencies. The exhaust gasses coming out of a Joule-Bryton cycle are quite hot, so they can be used to heat up the liquid in the Rankine cycle. This allows for very high efficiencies in the order of 60%. 

**==> picture [241 x 205] intentionally omitted <==**

## **Lecture 2** 

## **Brayton cycle** 

The assumptions of the ideal Brayton cycle are the following: 

- Ideal gas with constant Cp, Cv and chemical composition 

- Mass flow constant 

- Isentropic expansion and compression 

- No pressure losses in ducts or during heat supply 

5 

- No change in kinetic energy per each component (i.e. there is no need to use total quantities since the differences and ratios of total and static quantities coincide) 

If we consider the whole cycle, from the firs law: 

**==> picture [245 x 25] intentionally omitted <==**

The change in internal energy is zero since it’s a state variable and the initial and final thermodynamic state coincide. 

By again using the first law, the work (compression and expansion) and the heat (heat supply and rejection) coming in/out the system can be evaluated: 

**==> picture [261 x 92] intentionally omitted <==**

**==> picture [235 x 175] intentionally omitted <==**

With these relations a power balance equation can be setup: 

**==> picture [169 x 24] intentionally omitted <==**

Since the power extracted by the turbine is used to spin the compressor: 

**==> picture [115 x 68] intentionally omitted <==**

The heat we need to provide to the gas in the combustion chamber is equal to the power generated in the nozzle and the heat that is released into the environment by the exhaust gasses. 

By using these expressions, the thermodynamic efficiency of the Brayton cycle can be evaluated. This is defined as the ratio between the net power output and the total energy supply. 

6 

**==> picture [211 x 146] intentionally omitted <==**

Since compression and expansion are assumed to be isentropic, we can use the isentropic relations for temperature and pressure: 

**==> picture [153 x 55] intentionally omitted <==**

**==> picture [267 x 175] intentionally omitted <==**

We can see that the thermodynamic efficiency of the Joule-Brayton cycle is only dependent on the pressure ratio, the pressure difference between the max pressure (reached after the isentropic compression) and the min pressure (reached after the isentropic expansion). 

If we only cared about the maximum thermodynamic efficiency then we would need to maximize the pressure ratio and the ideal cycle would be the purple one. 

**==> picture [181 x 146] intentionally omitted <==**

However, additional factors need to be considered. The net power is defined as the total power generated in the expansion process, minus the power required to fuel the compressor: 

7 

**==> picture [197 x 43] intentionally omitted <==**

With a similar procedure as the one used to obtain the thermodynamic efficiency: 

**==> picture [183 x 62] intentionally omitted <==**

Where Ws,net is the specific net power, or the power generated per unit of mass flow, which is then normalized w.r.t. the ambient enthalpy. We can see that this parameter, which is as important as the thermodynamic efficiency is not only dependent on the pressure ration but also the temperature ratio. For an ideal cycle we have that the pressure ratio, for a given temperature ratio, has to be chosen at the optimum: 

**==> picture [249 x 192] intentionally omitted <==**

At a given pressure ratio, the higher the temperature ratio, the higher the specific power will, while the thermodynamic efficiency remains unchanged. 

At a given temperature ratio, there exists an optimum pressure ratio for which the specific net power is highest. When it comes to the efficiency it only depends on the pressure ratio and it is highest when the pressure ratio is highest. 

## **Real Brayton cycle** 

All of the previous results are based on the many assumptions listed at the beginning, which are: 

- Ideal gas with constant Cp, Cv and composition 

- Mass flow constant 

- Isentropic expansion and compression 

- No pressure losses in ducts or during heat supply 

- No change in kinetic energy per each component (no energy losses) 

Let’s look at the validity of all these assumptions. The assumption of constant Cp and Cv is not valid for a real cycle since they both depend on temperature and also composition. The addition of fuel in the combustion chamber changes the composition of the fluid, typically reducing the heat capacity ratio and increasing the heat capacity at constant pressure. 

8 

**==> picture [251 x 195] intentionally omitted <==**

This assumption can lead to over/underestimations of up to 5% for the efficiency of an engine. The addition of the fuel flow also breaks the assumption of constant mass flow rate. However, typically this addition is so small that it can be neglected. 

Another important assumption for the ideal Brayton cycle is that there are no kinetic energy changes in each component, however this is not true for the real cycle. To take into account these kinetic energy variations, since we do not know the velocity of the fluid in all the components, total quantities are used. 

**==> picture [138 x 50] intentionally omitted <==**

For a real Brayton cycle we also need to introduce efficiencies which take into account losses in both the compressor and the turbine. These efficiencies are defined as isentropic efficiencies since the ideal power is defined along an isentropic expansion/compression. 

**==> picture [169 x 48] intentionally omitted <==**

**==> picture [171 x 44] intentionally omitted <==**

For both compression and expansion, in the real case an increase in entropy is associated. For the real compressor more work is required to achieve the same pressure ratio, while for the turbine less work is extractable for the same pressure ratio. 

When solving a real Brayton cycle these efficiencies can be used to evaluate the final temperature/pressure reached. For the compressor: 

**==> picture [201 x 50] intentionally omitted <==**

For the turbine: 

**==> picture [139 x 47] intentionally omitted <==**

The total temperature at the turbine outlet is measured through a power balance with the compressor: 

9 

**==> picture [186 x 127] intentionally omitted <==**

In general as the pressure ratio increases the isentropic efficiency of the turbine increases as well, while the isentropic efficiency of the compressor decreases, so the benefits of increasing the pressure ratio are reduced. 

**==> picture [256 x 150] intentionally omitted <==**

Mechanical losses occur between the turbine and the compressor, so a mechanical efficiency can be defined (although it is typically very high ~99.5%). 

**==> picture [119 x 41] intentionally omitted <==**

Pressure losses occur in every component and have to be accounted for by introducing pressure ratios. 

**==> picture [151 x 86] intentionally omitted <==**

Overall the real Brayton cycle is: 

**==> picture [239 x 161] intentionally omitted <==**

Pressure losses in the inlet bring state 2 to a lower pressure. Losses in the compressor tilt the compression curve so that entropy increases and the power required increases. In the combustion 

10 

chamber, due to pressure losses, we move to a curve with lower pressure. In the turbine losses dictate an increase in entropy and a decrease in the total work that is extracted. Then pressure losses in the duct reduce the pressure from 5 to 7. Finally the expansion in the nozzle is also tilted to a higher entropy. 

By introducing losses the relations obtained for an ideal Brayton cycle break down. We see that for high pressure/temperature ratios they still roughly hold, but for lower temperature ratios this is not the case: 

**==> picture [219 x 191] intentionally omitted <==**

For lower temperature ratios, very little heat is being added into the system, while at the same time high pressure ratios are trying to be obtained. This is not possible since the cycle cannot maintain itself (work extracted is not enough to fuel the compression) and therefore both the net power and the efficiency go to zero. We also see that at constant pressure ratio now the thermodynamic efficiency increases slightly with temperature ratio. 

## **Lecture 3** 

## **Gas Generator** 

The "gas generator" part of the engine is the part which generates the **gas power** , or the power that is then transformed into kinetic energy (and then thrust). This transformation process into kinetic energy occurs after station g (in the nozzle). In particular, the gas power is the maximum amount of power that can be used, which is therefore defined along an isentropic expansion curve. 

**==> picture [265 x 121] intentionally omitted <==**

We can evaluate the power generated by this section as the gas power, or the power generated by the equivalent isentropic expansion from station g to 8’. 

11 

**==> picture [222 x 177] intentionally omitted <==**

**==> picture [112 x 25] intentionally omitted <==**

For a turbojet defining the part which generates the gas power is quite straightforward, since all the power extracted by the turbine is used only to power the compressor. Therefore the station g corresponds exactly with station 5 (turbine exit). However, a turbofan differs from a turbojet due to the presence of the fan in front of the compressor, which compresses both the bypass and the core flow. Thus the gas generator part is also different since part of the power extracted by the turbine is used to compress the bypass flow, which also generates kinetic energy. 

**==> picture [305 x 147] intentionally omitted <==**

In this case station g, where the gas generator ends, is positioned in the middle of the low pressure turbine. This is because the HPT drives the HPC which compresses only the core flow, while the LPT drives the booster (LPC) and the fan. The power used by the fan is divided in gas generation (compresses the core flow) and thrust generation (compresses the bypass flow). Therefore the station g is not exactly at the end of the LPT but slightly before. 

We also note that the part of the fan which compresses the core flow is part of the gas turbine. 

**==> picture [233 x 188] intentionally omitted <==**

**==> picture [123 x 26] intentionally omitted <==**

12 

Some useful definitions for aero engines (assuming ideal expansion conditions pe = pout): 

**Gross thrust** 𝐹𝐺 = 𝑚̇ 𝑣𝑗; The total force produced at the exhaust nozzle 

**Net thrust** 𝐹𝑁 = 𝑚̇ ∗(𝑣𝑗 −𝑣0) ; 

This is given as the gross thrust minus the **ram drag** , or the force needed to slow down the incoming air to speeds the engine can handle 

**==> picture [194 x 72] intentionally omitted <==**

Kinetic energy that can be transformed into thrust 

Given by the product of net thrust and flight velocity 

Residue kinetic energy at the nozzle exit that is not transformed into thrust 

**==> picture [276 x 126] intentionally omitted <==**

From the these equations we can see that we can increase the net thrust by increasing either the mass flow rate or the exhaust velocity. However, by increasing the exhaust velocity the power loss also increases with the square of the exhaust velocity, so it is much more convenient to increase the mass flow rate. This is the basic idea of the turbofan engine. 

If the nozzle is not in the perfect expansion condition the pressure at the nozzle exit is different from the environment pressure and this difference generates some thrust, which is proportional to the nozzle exit area and the pressure difference. This pressure difference can also be accounted for by defining an **effective jet velocity** vj,eff. 

**==> picture [243 x 70] intentionally omitted <==**

## **Aero engine efficiencies** 

Every station of the turbojet engine introduces losses that reduce the overall efficiency. 

13 

**==> picture [293 x 184] intentionally omitted <==**

- **Combustion efficiency** : the process that occurs is the transformation of chemical energy into heat. This takes into account the losses that occur in the combustion chamber due to incomplete combustion. The efficiency is given by extra amount of heat produced divided by the chemical energy contained in the fuel. 

**==> picture [257 x 35] intentionally omitted <==**

**==> picture [209 x 44] intentionally omitted <==**

- **Thermodynamic efficiency** : this determines the part of heat that is transformed into gas power and it’s given as the ratio between gas power and total heat produced in the combustion process. Takes into account the power used by the compressor. 

**==> picture [123 x 47] intentionally omitted <==**

Where the gas power can be evaluated as follows for a turbojet: 

**==> picture [312 x 182] intentionally omitted <==**

14 

- **Jet generation efficiency** : the process that occurs is the transformation of gas power into kinetic energy. This takes into account the losses that occur in the duct and nozzle during the expansion. 

**==> picture [129 x 43] intentionally omitted <==**

- **Propulsive efficiency** : the process that occurs is the transformation of kinetic energy into thrust power. This takes into account the kinetic energy that cannot be transformed into thrust. 

**==> picture [149 x 82] intentionally omitted <==**

- **Thermal efficiency** : given by the combination of the combustion, thermodynamic and jet generation efficiencies. This takes into account all the losses that occur in the engine. 

**==> picture [85 x 67] intentionally omitted <==**

- **Total efficiency** : takes into account all of the losses from the combustion chamber to the generation of thrust. It quantifies how efficiently the gas turbine transforms the chemical energy of the fuel into thrust power. 

**==> picture [91 x 38] intentionally omitted <==**

For a turbofan the same efficiencies can be defined and evaluated with some caveats: 

**==> picture [271 x 168] intentionally omitted <==**

- **Combustion efficiency** : defined and evaluated in the same way as we did for the turbojet. 

- • **Thermodynamic efficiency** : defined and evaluated in the same way as we did for the turbojet. 

However, the gas power is different in this case, since station g changes as well as the final temperature T8’’. Again the temperature at the station g is evaluated by setting up the power balance between the expansion from 4-g and the power needed to drive the core compressor (including the part of the fan that compresses the core flow). 

15 

To define the pressure at station g we need to evaluate it from the turbine efficiency and the temperature of the gasses at station g. 

Again the temperature at the exhaust after an isentropic expansion used to evaluate the gas power is obtained through the isentropic relations since p8’’=p8. 

**==> picture [269 x 154] intentionally omitted <==**

- **Jet generation efficiency** : defined in the same way as we did for the turbojet. In this case the kinetic energy not only takes into account the core flow but also the bypass flow. 

**==> picture [125 x 45] intentionally omitted <==**

- **Propulsive efficiency** : defined in the same way as we did for the turbojet. In this case the both flows need to be taken into account. 

**==> picture [137 x 39] intentionally omitted <==**

Overall for an unchoked nozzle we have: 

**==> picture [287 x 167] intentionally omitted <==**

Where the thermal efficiency can be obtained from the product of the combustion, thermodynamic and jet generation efficiencies. The global efficiency is still given by considering all of the losses encountered from the combustion chamber to the production of thrust. For an choked nozzle the jet velocity needs to be substituted by the effective jet velocity. For a generic jet engine we can visualize all of the losses through a Sankey diagram: 

16 

**==> picture [339 x 158] intentionally omitted <==**

Although the actual values might change from engine to engine, the largest amount of energy will always be lost through heat/to power the compressor and will be taken into account by the thermodynamic efficiency. Another common trend is the low losses due to incomplete combustion. 

Let’s now introduce some useful parameters to compare engines: 

**==> picture [248 x 111] intentionally omitted <==**

Specific thrust allows to quantify how efficiently an engine can generate thrust w.r.t. its size by dividing the net thrust by the mass flow rate. The thrust specific fuel consumption is instead given by the fuel flow ratio divided by the net thrust and it is inversely proportional to the global efficiency. These two values allow the comparison between engines of different sizes. 

## **Aero engine classification** 

Aero engines can be classified in many ways. One is to distinguish them based on how the gas power is used. 

**==> picture [283 x 129] intentionally omitted <==**

The gas power can either be converted into shaft power or kinetic energy. Both the turbofan and the turboprop actually lie between the two categories since they use the gas power to both generate shaft power and thrust. 

The other classification is based on the architecture of the engine. The simplest architecture is the single spool turbojet, which has been phased out by twin spool turbojets/turbofans (which have a high/low pressure compressor and a high/low pressure turbine) or even three spool turbofans. 

17 

This is because in the single spool turbojet, as the pressure increases the compressor stage loading is unbalanced. 

**==> picture [365 x 209] intentionally omitted <==**

The different spools allow each part of the compressor to run at their optimal speed. Turbofan engines can also be classified based on how large their bypass ratio (ratio between the mass flow rate in the fan and in the core) is. 

An additional engine that is outside of this classification is the Gear TurboFan engine GTF, a two spool turbofan with a gearbox which decouples the fan and the low pressure compressor. This engine has the advantage of a three spool turbofan while effectively only having two spools. The advantages of this decoupling will be explained in depth later. 

A similar classification can also be applied to turboshaft/prop engines. When the load is directly connected to both the compressor and the turbine it might drastically reduce the engine’s performance since their operational point is determined by the load and not by the turbomachinery optimal operating conditions. By inserting a free turbine which is only connected to the load this can be avoided and the compressor and HPT can both work at their optimal operating condition. 

**==> picture [285 x 161] intentionally omitted <==**

There are two other types of engines which follow the Brayton cycle that do not fit into these classifications : the ramjet and scramjet. These two types of engines do not have any mechanical components since they compress the air though dynamic compression while flying at supersonic speeds, yet they still follow the Joule-Brayton cycle. Therefore the compressor stage is not needed and neither the fan stage which drives it is. 

18 

These engines only work at high supersonic speeds at which air compresses dynamically in virtue of the high speeds. 

**==> picture [186 x 119] intentionally omitted <==**

**==> picture [235 x 111] intentionally omitted <==**

Different engine types have different operating envelopes as shown in the figure below. 

**==> picture [367 x 198] intentionally omitted <==**

The limits of this flight envelope are defined by different phenomena. At high flying altitudes and low speeds the limiting factor is the stall of the wings, while at low altitudes and high speeds it is the airframe/engine loads limits. 

**==> picture [253 x 169] intentionally omitted <==**

Another thing that distinguishes different engine architectures is the behaviour of propulsive efficiency to airspeed. In absolute terms, the turboprop has the highest propulsive efficiency at low speeds, however this drops drastically at transonic flight speeds due to tip shock on the propeller blades. The turbofan has the highest propulsive efficiency in the transonic and supersonic regimes, beating out the turbojet. We also observe that as the bypass ratio increases the propulsive efficiency also increases. 

19 

**==> picture [249 x 228] intentionally omitted <==**

## **Engine efficiency trends** 

In general we see an increase of all the efficiencies with the development of new technologies. The efficiency of aero engines increased by 70% in the past decades, while the thrust specific fuel consumption of aero engines decreased by 50%. 

The thrust specific fuel consumption reduction is due to the  improvements of engine component efficiency, cycle efficiency and a large part being the propulsive efficiency. The main contributors to this increase are the increased bypass ratio, TIT, compression ratio and the overall reduction of losses. 

**==> picture [301 x 205] intentionally omitted <==**

Nowadays typical values for the thermal efficiency are around 50-60%,  65-75% propulsive efficiency (for high bypass ratio turbofan engines, for which the bypass ratio has also doubled in the past decades) and total efficiencies of 40-50%. 

20 

**==> picture [302 x 213] intentionally omitted <==**

**Design considerations** 

To minimize the specific fuel consumption of new engines we want to maximize both the thermal efficiency and the propulsive efficiency, which are dependent on the following parameters: 

**==> picture [141 x 155] intentionally omitted <==**

**==> picture [113 x 46] intentionally omitted <==**

To increase the thermal efficiency we need to increase the efficiency of all engine components as well as the Overall Pressure Ratio OPR. For the Turbine Inlet Temperature TIT there is a specific value for which the thermal efficiency is maximized. 

Meanwhile for the propulsive efficiency we want to increase the ByPass Ratio BPR with a combined reduction of the Fan Pressure Ratio FPR. Also we want the TIT to decrease. 

We can see that we have opposing design goals, such as the increase in TIT to increase the thermal efficiency which leads to a decrease of propulsive efficiency. Therefore design choices need to be made. 

We can see how the design choices have lead to improvements over the years by comparing an engine from the ‘50s and a modern engine. 

We observe an increase in thrust generated, combined with a decrease in weight and in specific fuel consumption. This is due to the higher TIT and OPR while having fewer compressor stages. We also can observe an increase in diameter together with the BPR, while the overall length decreased. 

21 

**==> picture [289 x 208] intentionally omitted <==**

This analysis is valid for military engines, however similar trends can be observed for civil engines. 

**==> picture [339 x 203] intentionally omitted <==**

One small thing is that some newer engines are actually heavier than the old ones. This is due to the fact that fuel consumption (and emissions) has become one of the most important driving design parameters, while the thrust requirements have pretty much stayed the same. 

**==> picture [273 x 68] intentionally omitted <==**

A new introduction is the GTF engine which has a few advantages over conventional turbofans. 

22 

**==> picture [266 x 177] intentionally omitted <==**

An ultra-high bypass ratio can be achieved this way, since the fan speed can be drastically reduced thanks the gearbox, thus reducing the fan noise and increasing the propulsive efficiency. 

At the same time the LPT can rotate at much higher speeds, greatly increasing its efficiency while decreasing the number of stages needed and thus the weight. 

This reduction of fuel burn can only be achieved with this GTF architecture, since for a normal turbofan an increase in BPR eventually leads to higher fuel burn due to the increased weight of the LPT which has to spin at low speeds (and this requires more stages) since it’s coupled with the fan. 

**==> picture [277 x 155] intentionally omitted <==**

To increase the thermal efficiency we can increase the OPR. If we look at the trend over the years for the OPR this is exactly what we see, with modern engines having an OPR of around 50, almost double the OPR of engine from the ‘70s. 

The TIT also shows a general trend to increase over the years with modern TIT reaching values of 2000 K. This is actually much higher than the melting point of the materials used for the turbine blades, however this gap is bridged by using advanced blade cooling techniques. 

**==> picture [286 x 183] intentionally omitted <==**

23 

The BPR has had a much steeper increase over the last decades reaching values of 11-12, compared to BPRs of around 2-5 in the ’70s and ‘80s. At the same time the FPR has been decreasing. Further increases in the BPR however require new architectures, since the limit for the conventional turbofan has been reached. 

**==> picture [267 x 156] intentionally omitted <==**

The single components efficiencies have also been increasing, with HPC efficiencies reaching values of almost 90% in the last couple of years, while also having higher OPR and lower number of stages. The design trends for the compressor have been the following: 

**==> picture [201 x 126] intentionally omitted <==**

**==> picture [341 x 210] intentionally omitted <==**

**Future design trends** 

While older designs where mainly driven by increased thrust and lower fuel consumption, modern goals also include the reduction of NOx emissions, which tend to increase with OPR and TIT. We see that over the years the correlation between NOx emissions and OPR is decreasing thanks to better combustion technologies. 

24 

**==> picture [243 x 152] intentionally omitted <==**

The ACARE goal is the goal to cut in half NOx emissions by 2050. The main products which have environmental effects are the NOx and CO2 emissions, as well as **contrails** left behind by the engine. 

As we increase the design parameters of the engine (TIT, BPR and OPR) the fuel consumption and therefore the CO2 emissions decrease, while contrails formation and NOx emissions increase. 

Other limitations exist for the future engines. The fuel consumption is driven by thermal and propulsive efficiency. The former has a maximum theoretical limit due to limitations on the TIT (stochiometric TIT, above which combustion becomes less effective), on the efficiency of single components and on the OPR due to structural reasons. However, this limit is never going to be reached if we want to keep low NOx emission, as that will become the liming factor. The latter has a theoretical limit that is imposed for open rotor engines. 

**==> picture [237 x 169] intentionally omitted <==**

Overall however there is still room for improvement. The main design drivers for future development are going to be emissions, efficiency, energy sources and noise. 

## **Lecture 4** 

## **3 spool engine** 

As we can see from the image below the threes spool turbofan engine has three different shafts. The fan is connected to the low pressure turbine since it wants to rotate more slowly, the booster (LPC) is connected the intermediate pressure turbine and the HPT is connected to the HPC. 

25 

**==> picture [217 x 112] intentionally omitted <==**

This architecture is not very common and is mostly used by Rolls-Royce engines. The main advantage of this architecture is that the LPC (and therefore also the intermediate pressure turbine) is decoupled from the fan. In the design phase the fan is the most constricted component since the tip speed can easily reach critical speeds, increasing the noise produced and introducing shock losses in the fan exhaust (decreasing the overall efficiency of the engine). Therefore its rotational speed is strictly limited, which also limits the LPC in a two spool turbofan (due to the low pressure increase it can provide it’s typically called a “booster” stage). 

With three spools, the LPC is free to rotate faster and allow for higher OPR. 

Three spool architecture is also common for turboprops. 

**==> picture [261 x 114] intentionally omitted <==**

In this architecture the gearbox that drives the propeller is connected to a power turbine (free turbine), which is decoupled from the other turbines. The other two shafts connect the HPC to the HPT and the LPC to the LPT. 

The main advantage of having three spools for a turboprop is that it’s really easy to start the engine: the core is easier to start than the propeller, so by starting it, it can provide power to the power turbine, which drives the propeller. 

The other general advantage is the same that makes two spool engine more popular than one spool engines nowadays. The LPC and HPC have different optimal rotating speeds, so by decoupling them we can obtain a higher overall efficiency. 

The main disadvantage is the higher system complexity (thinner shafts, vibrations, ....). 

In summary the advantages and disadvantages of a three-spool turbofan engines are the following: 

**==> picture [297 x 170] intentionally omitted <==**

**Gear turbofan engine** 

26 

As we have seen before the GTF is a turbofan which has two main spools that connect the LPC and the LPT, as well as the HPT and the HPC. However, the GTF is different than conventional twin spool turbofans, because the fan and the LPC are decoupled by a gearbox. This solves the same issue that is solved by the three spool turbofan with a lower system complexity (although mechanical losses are introduced in the gearbox). This is why the GTF is called a 2.5 spool engine. 

The reduction ratio of the gearbox is around 2-2.5, which means that the fan is rotating at around half the speed of the LPC. This allows for higher OPR while avoiding tip shock at the fan. 

**==> picture [201 x 121] intentionally omitted <==**

GTF also has less LPT stages (around 3) compared to the 5-6 stages that normal twin spool turbofans have. This is possible thanks to the fact that the LPT can now rotate much faster, extracting the same amount of power from fewer turbine stages. The number of LPC is also generally lower since the increased rotational speed allows for higher pressure ratios for each stage. 

Revisiting the graph from the previous lecture, for a twin spool turbofan, after a certain BPR (around 10-11) the fuel consumption actually starts to increase. The positive effect of the increased BPR is negated by the decrease in efficiency of the fan and the LPC. 

This limit can be overcome by introducing a gearbox between the fan and the LPC. This allows to increase the BPR even more, while maintaining the gains on the fuel consumption. 

**==> picture [249 x 140] intentionally omitted <==**

Let’s now summarize the advantages of decoupling the fan and the LPC: 

- Low speed of the fan tips: leads to great noise reduction (as a rule of thumb the noise produced in the inlet is about proportional to the 4th power of the tip speed, while the noise produced by the nozzle is proportional to the 8th power of the nozzle speed. By increasing the BPR we can choose which contribution to reduce) 

- Optimum speed for the fan and the LPC: this increases the efficiency and lower the fuel consumption 

- High speed LPC and LPT: this allows the reduction in LPT stages (to about half)  while increasing the pressure ratio of the LPC at the same time. From the figure we can see that for the same expansion ratio the number of stages required is drastically less for a geared architecture compared to a conventional architecture. 

27 

**==> picture [193 x 152] intentionally omitted <==**

These benefits come at the price of increased weight and system complexity. Also the larger BPR and engine diameter might lead to an increase in installation penalty  (aerodynamic losses caused by the interaction between the nacelle-pylon-wing could increase. This is caused by the larger engine, which can lead to a shorter pylon to satisfy ground clearance requirements). 

Due to the higher LPT rotation the turbine blades have higher requirements, since the centripetal forces acting on them are increased. 

Finally the heat rejection of the engine becomes complicated  (increased cooling system complexity and requirements). 

A big challenge in designing the gearbox is maximizing the mechanical efficiency. This is not only too reduce power losses but also to reduce the cooling requirements. 

Note: GTF engine have the lowest specific fuel consumption between all the different architectures. A three spool engine is slightly less efficiency since the turbine coupled with the fan still has to rotate at a slower velocity, which is not the case for a GTF. 

From a smith chart we can see that a reduction in rotational velocity increases the stage loading (total enthalpy jump divided by the square of the velocity) and flow coefficient (proportional to the flow velocity), thus reducing the efficiency. Typically we want high velocity to reduce the stage loading and flow coefficient. See later lectures on turbomachinery. 

**==> picture [261 x 178] intentionally omitted <==**

## **Afterbuner** 

The afterburner is a long secondary combustion chamber, which is added to military turbojets/turbofan engines to reheat the exhaust gasses that come out of the turbine to generate more thrust in the subsequent nozzle (typically a convergent-divergent nozzle with a variable geometry). 

28 

**==> picture [230 x 109] intentionally omitted <==**

Since we are reheating the gasses, the pressure slightly drops due to pressure losses, while the temperature increases rapidly to a temperature that is higher than the one reached in the primary combustion chamber. This is possible because the TIT is limited by the heat resistance of the turbine blades, however there are no turbomachinery components behind the afterburner. 

**==> picture [217 x 122] intentionally omitted <==**

From the T-S diagram of the ideal cycle with afterburner we can quickly see that the power generated (and thus thrust, which is typically increased by 40-50%) is much higher since the expansion process from 7 to 8 is much longer than from 4 to 5. This comes at the cost of an enormous reduction to the efficiency of the engine and increased length. 

If we consider afterburning turbofans, we see that they typically have a lower BPR than conventional turbofans. The bypass flow is mixed with the exhaust gasses coming from the turbine before the reheating process occurs. 

To summarize the features of an afterburning engine are: 

- The specific thrust can be increased instantaneously 

- The thrust to weight ratio is very high 

- The off design performance can be significantly increased without penalizing the design point performance (e.g. short take off, evading manoeuvres) 

- The diameter of the engine is not increased 

These come at some disadvantages: 

- The specific fuel consumption is increased significantly (up to 300%) when using after burner. 

- The length of the engine is more than a conventional engine 

- The weight of the engine is more than a conventional engine 

- The infrared signature is increased immensely when using afterburner 

- There is a need for variable area nozzle with using afterburner 

## **Inter-turbine burner engine** 

The idea at the basis of the inter-turbine burner engine is that we can insert the secondary combustion chamber ahead of the LPT instead of after it. 

29 

**==> picture [298 x 117] intentionally omitted <==**

Just like the afterburner the inter-turbine burner is burning fuel at an higher  initial temperature (when compared to the main combustion chamber), however in virtue of the fact that it is doing it at a higher pressure w.r.t. the afterburner, the thermodynamic efficiency of the process is much higher (although it is lower than that of the main combustor). This occurs because increasing the temperature of a gas at a higher pressure is more thermodynamically efficient than doing it at a lower pressure (at higher pressure a bigger part of the added energy through heat can be transformed into useful kinetic energy and the entropy generation is less). This can also be seen directly from the T-S diagram. In conclusion this engine can produce the higher thrust than a conventional turbojet at a lower fuel “cost” than a conventional afterburner, however the process is still less efficient than a normal turbojet which produces the same thrust. 

**==> picture [231 x 138] intentionally omitted <==**

Thanks to the presence of an additional burner, the cycle can be optimized in many ways. If the goal is to maximize the specific thrust produced, then the thermodynamic cycle would probably look like the following: 

**==> picture [223 x 152] intentionally omitted <==**

The amount of fuel added to the ITB has to so that the end temperature is the same as the TIT. By doing so the specific power of the system is maximized. This is because the temperature at the exit of the ITB is still limited by the LPT blades thermal resistance. By going as close as possible to this limit we maximize the power produced in the nozzle. 

However, if we want to minimize the NOx emissions, then the cycle would look like: 

30 

**==> picture [249 x 175] intentionally omitted <==**

To reduce the NOx emissions we want to lower the TIT (NOx production increases almost exponentially with the combustion temperature after a certain threshold). To compensate for the lower TIT, we rise the temperature in the ITB, allowing for similar thrust generation (although at a higher TSFC). 

Another effect which leads to NOx emissions reduction is the dissociation of NOx produced by the first combustor in the second combustor. 

**==> picture [245 x 138] intentionally omitted <==**

The volume of the ITB has to be higher than the volume of the first combustion chamber because the gasses are at a lower pressure, so to obtain the similar residence time (amount of time the gas stays in the combustion chamber to absorb heat) we need a larger volume. 

Another advantage of the ITB is the possibility of lowering the TIT for the same thrust generation. 

**==> picture [237 x 178] intentionally omitted <==**

This allows for a lower amount of cooling air, which is typically needed since the HPT blades are operating at higher temperature than their melting point. This air is taken from the compressor, effectively reducing the overall efficiency of the engine. By reducing the amount of air taken from the compressor, the overall efficiency is further increased. 

31 

**==> picture [203 x 134] intentionally omitted <==**

Note: the ITB energy fraction refers to how much of the fuel is allocated to the ITB instead of the main combustion chamber. 

Obviously the disadvantages of this architecture is the increased size, weight and system complexity (it is very hard to design the ITB so that complete combustion is achieved). 

To recap: 

**==> picture [300 x 148] intentionally omitted <==**

## **Recuperated gas turbine engine** 

Let’s first look at an industrial example of a gas turbine. 

**==> picture [301 x 140] intentionally omitted <==**

To increase the thermodynamic efficiency we can take the exhaust gasses, which are typically at a higher temperature than the gasses passing from the compressor to the combustion chamber, to heat the gas before it enters the combustor. This reduces the required heat that the combustion chamber has to provide, thus increasing the efficiency. This process is called **recuperation** . 

32 

**==> picture [171 x 146] intentionally omitted <==**

The recuperation is most effective when the pressure ratio is low, since more of the heat can be extracted from the exhaust gasses. 

In an industrial power plant recuperation occurs after the expansion, however in an aero engine the recuperation can only occur before the expansion in the nozzle. This is not as efficient as the recuperation in the industrial gas turbine since the process of recuperation introduces temperature and pressure losses which affect the flow in the nozzle. 

**==> picture [267 x 116] intentionally omitted <==**

Recuperation has never been implemented in an aero engine also because it disturbs the air before it comes into the nozzle, reducing thrust generated. Also the weight and size required to implement such a concept is not compatible with civil aviation standards. 

**==> picture [235 x 141] intentionally omitted <==**

**Lecture 5 Intercooling and recuperation** 

**==> picture [229 x 131] intentionally omitted <==**

33 

In order to reduce the amount of work required by the HPC, **intercooling** can be implemented. The air after the LPC is taken to an intercooler, where it is cooled through a heat exchanger filled with either water or surrounding air. Once the temperature of the air is reduced, it is then fed to the HPC. This significantly reduces the work required for the same pressure jump, since it is “cheaper” (it cost less power) to compress a fluid a lower temperature. This greatly increases the efficiency of the cycle. 

**==> picture [230 x 157] intentionally omitted <==**

A downside to intercooling can be directly seen from the T-S diagram. Since the temperature of the fluid is now colder when it enters the combustion chamber more heat is going to have to be added into the system. This does decrease the specific net power. 

In the following example the core air after the LPC is intercooled by using the bypass air. 

**==> picture [242 x 161] intentionally omitted <==**

Since as we have previously seen the amount of NOx produced in the combustion chamber is proportional to the temperature at which the combustion process occurs, by lowering the temperature the emissions of NOx can also be reduced. 

Intercooling however it’s not used in aero engine due to the increased engine weight and volume. Moreover, the pressure losses that occur in the heat exchangers are typically very high and destroy almost all gains that come from the lower power required by the compressor. Also the decrease of the combustor inlet temperature decreases the specific thrust generated by the engine (although it does also decrease the TSFC). 

Intercooling can actually be combined with recuperation, since the second process is actually limited by the temperature difference between the exit air at the HPC and the exhaust coming out of the LPT. The intercooling reduces the first temperature, increasing the temperature difference and making recuperation possible and more effective. 

34 

**==> picture [267 x 131] intentionally omitted <==**

**==> picture [233 x 147] intentionally omitted <==**

In a way recuperation also negates the disadvantage of intercooling (lower combustor inlet temperature) since it reduces the amount of heat that needs to be transferred to the gas. If we applied these concepts to a turbofan: 

**==> picture [281 x 162] intentionally omitted <==**

From conceptual designs we know that the expected temperature rise of air after the HPC in the recuperator would be in the order of 200 K, while the temperature drop in the intercooler would be around 70 K. Also the heat exchanged with the bypass air adds energy to it and gets accelerated. This third flow of cooling air could also be discharged by a third nozzle increasing overall thrust. 

In the heat exchangers there are significant pressure losses, which can actually be offset by the temperature rise in the recuperator. However, pressure losses are a major limiting factor and in some cases can negate all the benefits. Also system weight is increased, while reliability is reduced. 

Since recuperation is typically more efficient than intercooling (the temperature jumps are different) the temperature at which combustion occurs can actually become higher and therefore the NOx emissions could also increase. 

## **Hybrid electric propulsion** 

35 

As we have seen previously the main components of a propulsion system are the energy source, the energy to work converter and the thrust producer. 

Electric aviation is one of the many options for alternative propulsion systems and use batteries as the energy source, electric motors as energy to work convertors and typically propellers as thrust generators. Let’s look at how efficient a system of this kind is: 

**==> picture [303 x 198] intentionally omitted <==**

Every step of the process introduces small losses (which lead to a global efficiency of around 60%), which overall are better than the losses that occur inside a conventional aero engine. 

One of the main constraints when it comes to electric energy propulsion is the energy density of batteries. 

**==> picture [241 x 168] intentionally omitted <==**

At the cell level the specific energy density and the volumetric energy density are not too bad actually, however, at the system level (along with cooling systems and other systems needed) the energy density drops. Nevertheless, even at the cell level, the volumetric energy density of kerosene is still around 40 times that of the best batteries. 

Another major limitation is the battery life of current batteries. If we were to use completely a battery each recharge cycle (meaning we use their full capacity) we see that the number of cycles for which they can be used is greatly reduced and not satisfactory. 

36 

**==> picture [285 x 173] intentionally omitted <==**

While for a car such battery life can be acceptable, since they do not need to charged that often, for a plane, which needs to be charged on average 5-6 times a day this is unacceptable. To offset these drawbacks, the implementation of electric energy can also be done together with another form of energy. Fuel cell electric systems use hydrogen, which is passed through a condenser and is fed into a fuel cell, together with air coming from a compressor. Inside the fuel cell chemical energy is transformed into electrical energy which then power an electric motor. The efficiency line looks like: 

**==> picture [335 x 208] intentionally omitted <==**

By adding this one step to the thrust generation process the total efficiency drop to around ~20%. However, this does partially solve the two issue of pure electric power systems. In summary, electric propulsion systems have the following advantages: 

- Higher efficiency, especially for systems which use batteries 

- Low emissions 

- Lower noise (only regarding the component that generate the useful work, the propeller still creates a large amount of noise) 

- Architectural flexibility (can be used for many different aircraft architectures) 

- Reduction in the control surface area 

- One engine out condition is not a constraint anymore 

- Possibility of thrust vectoring 

- Suited for urban mobility 

However, there are a few disadvantages: 

- The energy density of electrical components is not great 

- The propulsion system weight is high 

- The integration of propulsion systems with the aircraft can become complex 

37 

- The reliability of such systems is not known yet 

- Electromagnetic interference is an open issue 

- Lightning strikes and other issues need to be investigated 

Yet another option is to integrate electrical power systems in conventional aero engines. 

Hybrid electric architecture can be classified as follows: 

**==> picture [387 x 179] intentionally omitted <==**

In the **all turboelectric engine** , the gas turbine is not producing any thrust, but only electric energy power which is then used to drive propellers. This is basically a turboprop. 

A battery can be added in series to the turboshaft to aid in power generation to drive the propellers. Depending on the flight phase the power generated by the turboshaft can be used to recharge the batteries. The batteries in this case act as buffers to smoothen out transition phases and can help to reduce the total emissions. This architecture is called the **series hybrid electric** . 

If the batteries are added in parallel then typically we have a turbofan instead of a turboshaft. In this case the battery is driving an electrical motor, which is assisting a gas turbine (it can boost the efficiency in many ways by providing energy to all components). This architecture is called the **parallel hybrid electric** . 

Finally, a combination of the two previous system can be obtained. Batteries are assisting a turbofan, which is producing both thrust and electrical energy which is used to drive propellers or recharging the batteries, depending on the flight phase. This architecture is called the **series/parallel hybrid electric** . 

**==> picture [384 x 206] intentionally omitted <==**

Note: for the HEPS the fan is decoupled from the compressors, therefore the overall efficiency of the engine is greatly increased. 

38 

In the series architecture the fan and the core are completely separated. 

An example of HEPS is the NASA N3-X, a concept aircraft with a blended wing-body, distributed propulsion and hybrid electric power systems. 

**==> picture [288 x 125] intentionally omitted <==**

The gas turbine engines are at the tips of the wings and they only produce power, which is then used to run smaller electric engine that are distributed over the body. 

This distributed propulsion systems have quite a few advantages, such as: 

- Higher effective bypass ratio 

- Lower noise 

- Better suited for boundary layer ingestion 

- Architectural flexibility 

- Reduction in the control surface area 

- One engine out condition is not a constraint anymore 

- Possibility of thrust vectoring 

As we can see the advantages match up with the advantages of HEPS. The disadvantages are: 

- More complex system architecture 

- The integration with the aircraft becomes more complex 

- The reliability of such systems is not known 

- The energy density of electrical components is not good 

- Electromagnetic interference is an open issue 

- Lightning strikes and other issues need to be investigated 

An example of parallel HEPS engines are electrically assisted turbofans, which feature an electric motor that drives the fan. This motor brings great advantages during take-off, climb, descent and taxiing. Thus for short range aircraft the efficiency is increased by quite a bit, meanwhile for longer ranges no particular benefit are obtained due to the increase weight of the system and low energy density of batteries. 

## **Open rotor propulsion** 

An open rotor engine, also known as a **propfan** is an aero engine which combines features of turbofans and turboprops to obtain the thrust capabilities of a turbofan in lower transonic regimes (M~0.7), while maintaining turboprop efficiency. 

39 

**==> picture [204 x 193] intentionally omitted <==**

Although an interesting concept propfan engines never caught on mainly due to the great cabin noise they produce when compared to normal turbofans. 

The main advantages of such an aero engine architecture are: 

- Large savings in fuel burn due to the large BPR (80-90) 

- Very low FPR, no duct and low downstream swirl when compared to conventional turboprops 

- Reduced nacelle drag 

These come at a cost: 

- Noise emission is greatly increased when compared to a normal turbofan 

- New engine installation needed with unprecedented issues 

- Balde containment issues 

- Power transmission to rotors (introduced mechanical losses) 

Note: engine design cycle 

**==> picture [287 x 219] intentionally omitted <==**

## **Lecture 6** 

## **Engine intake** 

The design criteria of the inlets can be classified into 5 main types: 

40 

- **Aerodynamics** : the main requirement of an inlet is that it has to provide the adequate mass flow rate to the engine with minimal pressure loss and minimum distortion. The **installation penalty** (interaction between nacelle, pylon and wing) also has to be minimized. Finally, the thrust growth is also an important factor. When designing a family of aircrafts (e.g. A320) the thrust growth has to be taken into account, since future aircraft will require more powerful engines and ideally this wouldn’t require a whole new design for the intake. 

- **Structural** : the objective is to minimize the weight and the cost. 

- **Noise** : the objective is to reduce fan noise. The important elements are the acoustic liner inside the intake and the management of the boundary layer. 

- **Safety** : foreign object damage is particularly important for military aircrafts. Ground clearance and anti icing systems drive the design of the intake. An example of the importance of ground clearance is the intake of the Boeing 737 NG, which has an oval shape to maximize the mass flow rate coming into the engine, while maintaining a certain clearance. 

- **Stealth** (only important for military aircrafts): we want to minimize the radar cross section, the infrared emissions and the frontal area. 

**==> picture [257 x 182] intentionally omitted <==**

Note: **foreign object damage** FOD refers to object present on the runway that get accidentally ingested by the engine and can greatly damage the engine. The intake has to be designed to minimize the risk of foreign object ingestion. An extreme example of an inlet designed to minimize the risk of FOD, are the top inlets of the Mig 29, which can provide enough mass flow rate for the aircraft to take off with the main inlets closed. This feature can also be used during high speed manoeuvring to increase the stall margin of the compressor. 

**==> picture [164 x 126] intentionally omitted <==**

41 

**==> picture [291 x 169] intentionally omitted <==**

Some important considerations for inlets: 

- The intake has to always provide the required mass flow rate in every condition. Therefore the main flight conditions which drive the inlet design are the take-off condition and the cruise condition. 

- During cruise one important factor to take into consideration is the **ram effect** , or dynamic compression of the fluid. Since the inlet slows down the entering air to around M=0.6 at the fan face, some kinetic energy is transformed into potential energy. This translates into an increase in static pressure (while total pressure decreases slightly due to losses). 

- Intake can be distinguished into subsonic and supersonic intakes. For the former, boundary layer behaviour is of the utmost importance, while for the latter the shock structure through which the flow is slowed down to subsonic conditions is much more important. Another important difference is that for a subsonic flow a divergent duct decelerates it, while for a supersonic flow a convergent duct decelerates it. 

**==> picture [88 x 24] intentionally omitted <==**

Another example of inlet design mainly driven by one discipline is the inlets of the F117 nighthawk, which are mounted over the wing and are protected by a mesh. All of these measures are taken to improve the stealth of the aircraft. 

The classic schematic of an engine nacelle can be seen in the figure below. 

**==> picture [290 x 222] intentionally omitted <==**

**==> picture [9 x 9] intentionally omitted <==**

**----- Start of picture text -----**<br>
2<br>**----- End of picture text -----**<br>


42 

We see that the **thrust reverser** system is integrated with the engine nacelle. These systems (which can be of bucket, cascade or plume type) reverse part of (or all of) the flow to create reverse thrust and aid the breaking on icy/wet runways. These systems cannot however be used to certify landing distance on dry runways due to their low reliability. 

Nevertheless, they are used to reduce break wear. Most of the modern thrust reverses on civil a/c are of the cascade type and reverse only the fan flow. 

Another important part of the nacelle is the **inlet cowl** . This component is lined on the inside with a material that absorbs acoustic waves to reduce the noise produced called **acoustic liner** . Also inside the inlet cowl is a heating system, which is part of the pneumatic system, and prevents the formation of ice in the intake, which could get ingested by the engine. 

The geometry of the cowling can be defined by total diameter of the nacelle, the diameter of the highlight (which is the “leading edge” of the airfoil that makes up the cowling), the ramp angle and the diameter of the throat. The last two parameters define the **mass flow rate ratio** of the intake MFR: 

**==> picture [56 x 26] intentionally omitted <==**

Where 𝐴∞ is the cross section of the stream tube that gets ingested by the intake. In the two conditions that drive the design of the inlet we have: 

- **Cruise** : low MFR, which means that the stream tube expands over the cowling, leading to an increased risk of shock outside the cowling. The design for this phase focuses mainly on the external geometry. For mass flow rates different than the design value (lower values for example), the stream tube ingested might be slightly larger, leading to spillage drag and possible separation on the nacelle. 

**==> picture [237 x 132] intentionally omitted <==**

- **Take-off** : high MFR, which means that the stream tube converges into the intake and separation can occur on the inside of the cowling. The design of this phase focuses mainly on the internal geometry. 

**==> picture [211 x 150] intentionally omitted <==**

The pressure and Mach number variations in the inlet are as follows: 

43 

**==> picture [270 x 188] intentionally omitted <==**

The inlet geometry controls the balance between: 

- **External diffusion** (ahead of the nacelle): at cruise, part of the diffusion occurs outside the nacelle and is nearly adiabatic, reducing internal losses (there is no wall friction). 

- **Internal diffusion** (inside the inlet): occurs in virtue of the converging geometry of the inlet (in the subsonic case). This is where the pressure losses are introduced. 

This diffusion can be seen in the Mach number and static pressure trends depicted in the figure above. The flow is slowed down and the pressure increases. 

To take into account losses that occur during ram compression a **ram efficiency** (or **ram recovery factor** ) can be defined, while an **isentropic efficiency** can be defined to take into account losses in mechanical energy w.r.t. an ideal compression (i.e. isentropic). 

The fist is defined as the ratio between the difference between the total and static pressure at the fan face and the difference between the total and static pressure at the intake entrance. The second one is the defined as the ratio between the real enthalpy jump that occurs across the compression and the ideal enthalpy jump that would occur across an isentropic compression. 

Up until M = 0.8 the two are pretty much equivalent, however the introduction of shocks at higher Mach numbers changes things and the ram recovery factor is preferred (ratio between the total pressure after the inlet and before). Empirical relations can be used to evaluate the ram recovery factor (typical values of 0.6-0.7) in supersonic conditions: 

**==> picture [141 x 15] intentionally omitted <==**

**==> picture [287 x 167] intentionally omitted <==**

44 

At take-off since the flight speed can be approximated to zero, the total pressure is equal to the ambient static pressure and there is almost no ram effect. In this case the inlet pressure ratio is basically 1 and the intake acts like a **Bellmouth inlet** . 

**==> picture [275 x 164] intentionally omitted <==**

A Bellmouth inlet  is a rounded inlet with a trumpet-shaped entrance that has a gradual increase in cross-section from free stream to duct. This special geometry guides streamlines smoothly into the duct and prevents flow separation at the inlet lip, thus reducing inlet losses. In ideal conditions it has almost no pressure losses and that is why it is used in wind tunnel tests to achieve uniform flow at the fan face, maximizing mass flow rate. Nevertheless, they are not actually used since they have very high drag and poor off-design performance. 

**==> picture [275 x 153] intentionally omitted <==**

The curvature of the inside of the inlet throat causes a non uniform velocity profile. This is why we typically use an average value for the Mach number in the inlet and at the fan face. The higher the curvature the stronger the velocity gradients and the more the flow will be non uniform at the fan face. The shape of the lip has to be a compromise between resistance to separation at low speeds and wave drag control at high speeds. 

**==> picture [262 x 159] intentionally omitted <==**

45 

All of this is particularly important for off-design operations such as low speed operations with high crosswind. These conditions can cause flow separation at the lip and drastically reduce the mass flow rate provided to the engine and cause the compressor to surge. Another issue that needs to be considered is flow distortion which can be quantified through distortion parameters. These are used to evaluate the operability margins. 

## **Installation effects** 

As aviation has evolved we can clearly see a tendency for the engine sizes to increase. This is to accommodate for higher and higher BPR. 

Engine can be mounted on the wing in two different ways: 

**==> picture [321 x 132] intentionally omitted <==**

In the second case, to maintain the required ground clearance the position of the engine is shifted forward and up so that interference drag doesn’t increase drastically. This is the most common solution. 

When we talk about installation penalties we are referring to the interactions between all of the components that are in close proximity to the installation point of the engine. Interference effects between the nacelle-pylon-wing/fuselage can lead to great super-velocities and therefore increased drag and risk of shock-induced separation. 

However, this is not the only drag component the inlet design influences. The drag generated by a nacelle can be divided into: 

- **Profile drag** : the air flow has move around the nacelle. This creates pressure and friction drag known as profile drag. This is the biggest contributor to total nacelle drag. 

- **Spillage drag** : when the mass flow rate is different than the design mass flow rate, the stream tube coming into the engine has to converge/diverge more. This can lead to mass flow rate spillage over the nacelle which increases drag. 

- **Wave drag** : super-velocities over the nacelle can lead to shocks and shock-induced separation 

- • **Windmilling drag** : this drag component is only relevant when the engine is inoperative and the mass flow rate through it is drastically lower than in normal operating conditions. The windmilling drag component takes into account the increase in drag from the OEI condition. 

- **Additive drag** : is the pressure drag generated from the pressure difference between the air inside the stream tube and the air around it. 

- **Interference drag** : the interaction of the flow around the components that are in close proximity to each other leads to an increase in super-velocities and therefore drag. 

46 

**==> picture [289 x 133] intentionally omitted <==**

The main design parameter that effect drag generation of the nacelle is obviously the nacelle diameter. As BPR increases, all of the drag components tend to increase, so the gains brought on the TSFC are slightly offset by this increase in drag. This balance is what determines the optimal BPR of an engine. 

**==> picture [279 x 155] intentionally omitted <==**

In civil aircrafts the installation of an engine on the wing can vary greatly: 

**==> picture [317 x 206] intentionally omitted <==**

These movements are obviously constrained. For example, the axial position of the engine is determined by both the requirements on interference drag and blade off containment, the vertical position effects the ground clearance, the spanwise position is determined by the tail size for the OEI condition and so on. The pitch and toe angle mainly affect the total thrust and the stability. The general design criteria for determining the engine installation position are: 

47 

**==> picture [273 x 145] intentionally omitted <==**

Another important aspect that needs to be taken into account when choosing the engine installation position is the fact that the presence of the nacelle “spoils” the flow behind it that goes over the wing. This reduces the local lift produced and can be observed from the spanwise distribution of lift. This can be offset by adding fins on the engine nacelle which generate stable vortices. 

**==> picture [220 x 143] intentionally omitted <==**

In conclusion, while for an isolated turbofan an increase in BPR to very high values can lead to great fuel consumption improvements, in reality the installation penalty determines a much lower optimal BPR to minimize fuel consumption. 

**==> picture [303 x 175] intentionally omitted <==**

**Intake classification** 

Inlets can be classified in many different ways. 

48 

**==> picture [348 x 242] intentionally omitted <==**

Military aircrafts typically use supersonic, conformal (meaning that the inlet is flush with the aircraft geometry), rectangular/ Y-shaped and variable geometry inlets. For civil aircrafts the inlets are instead podded, circular and have a fixed geometry. 

The **inlet efficiency** (the losses of pressure that occur in the inlet) varies over the mission profile. The inlet can be designed to optimize one of the phases of a typical mission; for example supersonic inlets tend to have very thin lips to avoid shocks over the cowling during cruise. This however greatly reduces the inlet performance at lower Mach numbers since separation can occur on the inside lip. If we look at two different inlets developed for the F-16 (one with variable geometry and one not), we see that the variable geometry inlet is able to maximize the pressure ratio over a larger operational envelope. 

**==> picture [183 x 146] intentionally omitted <==**

Despite the better performance the F-16 was eventually fitted with the fixed geometry inlet due to the lower system complexity-cost-maintenance. 

Inlet efficiency is extremely important, since for every percentage loss we have an overall decrease in the performance of the engine of about .5%. 

## **Inlet distortion** 

Let’s consider a Y-shaped inlet, in other words an inlet which has two intakes , which join together to provide the engine with only one mass flow rate. 

49 

**==> picture [267 x 163] intentionally omitted <==**

In this kind of duct a prominent boundary layer forms in both the branches and does not disappear when they merge. This, together with the peculiar geometry, creates a non uniform pressure and velocity distribution. 

This leads to an erratic behaviour in the pressure recovery coefficient with respect to sideslip angle 

**==> picture [341 x 196] intentionally omitted <==**

This means that the engine is facing non uniform flow at the inlet. If we look at the pressure profile along the inlet walls we see high and low pressure regions. A **distortion parameter** can be defined to quantify the difference between the max pressure and min pressure in any 60 degree section w.r.t. the dynamic pressure. 

**==> picture [303 x 161] intentionally omitted <==**

When the engine is facing non uniform flow at the inlet, the compressor characteristics change. In particular the surge line of the compressor map comes down, which reduces the operational margin of the engine (increasing the risk of surge in the engine and catastrophic accidents). 

50 

**==> picture [262 x 182] intentionally omitted <==**

The way distortion travels around the inlet can be tested with a fan test rig by putting a distortion screen over a certain 60 degree region in front of the fan. This screen creates located distortion, that allows us to evaluate the effects of the distortion on the duct, fan and compressor. 

**==> picture [329 x 187] intentionally omitted <==**

From the image on the right we can clearly see that distortion in the inlet makes the flow in the duct non-uniform and this introduces losses in all the downstream components. 

Distortion can come from many sources. On some novel aircraft architectures distortion at the inlet can come from boundary layer ingestion (e.g. Boundary layer ingestion inlets prototypes). 

One big issue created by distortion is the fact that once the flow is not uniform anymore, asymmetrical loads on the fan appear. This can lead to cyclical loads that promote fatigue. A way to reduce the distortion effects is to increase the inlet length. This allows the flow to become more stable by the time it reaches the fan. 

## **Supersonic inlets** 

For supersonic inlets the difference between the static pressure and the total pressure increases as the Mach number increases. As Mach increases also this difference cannot be evaluated with Bernoulli’s equation since compressibility and shocks invalidate the assumptions. We can see the Bernoulli prediction deviate more and more as Mach number increases above M = 1. 

51 

**==> picture [239 x 159] intentionally omitted <==**

For very high Mach numbers, the difference between static and total pressure difference becomes so big, that when the flow is slowed down to subsonic velocities in the inlet, the static pressure increases drastically and the compressor has to do little to no work (ram compression). 

The geometry of the duct is the factor which has the most influence on the flow behaviour in an inlet. We see that the flow behaves differently depending on the regime: 

**==> picture [237 x 207] intentionally omitted <==**

We see that supersonic flow behaves in an opposite manner to subsonic flow. Thermodynamic properties always change in the opposite way to the velocity. 

As an example let’s consider a supersonic convergent-divergent inlet. The supersonic flow slows down in the convergent part of the inlet and reaches the throat in sonic conditions. Now since it’s subsonic, in the divergent part the flow will continue to diffuse, exiting the duct in subsonic conditions. This geometry is what is typically used for supersonic inlets. 

**==> picture [261 x 88] intentionally omitted <==**

One of the applications of this kind of inlet is the ramjet engine, in which all of the compression is done by the ram compression. The compressor is therefore taken out, together with the turbine which drives it. In high supersonic regime these kinds of engines are the most efficient, however they cannot work in sonic/subsonic conditions. Both the inlet and the nozzle have a convergent-divergent 

52 

geometry, one to slow down supersonic flow to subsonic conditions and the other to speed back up to supersonic conditions. 

**==> picture [216 x 130] intentionally omitted <==**

One of the possible geometries of such an inlet is the **spike geometry** . In supersonic conditions, the shock waves attach to the tip of the spike. Then the position of the spike is determined so that these shockwaves all attach to the lip of the cowling ensuring maximum mass flow rate with very little spillage. A system of oblique shocks gradually slows down the flow until a last normal shock inside the inlet brings it to subsonic conditions. 

**==> picture [229 x 161] intentionally omitted <==**

For this kind of inlet we can define three operational regimes: 

- **Subcritical** : the Mach number is lower than the design value. The normal shock occurs ahead of the cowl lip increasing the spillage drag. 

- • **Critical** : the Mach number is equal to the design value. The normal shock is located at the throat. 

- **Supercritical** : the Mach number is higher than the operational Mach number. The shock moves aft but the total mass flow rate into the engine stays the same. 

To avoid the increase in spillage drag that occurs in the subcritical condition due to the normal shock being outside of the inlet we can have a variable geometry inlet, for which the spike position can be moved forward or aft. This type of systems typically also have a bypass system that allows for spillage of the mass flow rate in the inlet to control the position of the normal shock. 

**==> picture [91 x 195] intentionally omitted <==**

**==> picture [317 x 73] intentionally omitted <==**

53 

Another way to control the normal shock position is through active flow control (providing suction through the skin of the inlet). 

One of the main issues of fixed convergent-divergent inlets is **unstart** . For a fixed geometry inlet, the C-D duct chokes at a specific Mach number and the position of the last normal shock changes as the Mach number changes. In these conditions small perturbations can actually make it so that the normal shock comes out of the inlet forming a bow shock in front of it. This increases the spillage drag greatly and reduces the incoming mass flow rate to the engine decreasing overall thrust.
