Having a strong normal shock in the inlet creates great pressure losses since the compression that occurs across it is not isentropic but is instead associated with a great increase in entropy. Therefore to reduce pressure losses we prefer to have a system of oblique shocks followed by a weak normal shock. To have this we can have an inlet which has different ramps with different ramp angles, each creating an oblique shock. 

When the oblique shocks occur only outside the inlet (bow shock followed by a series of oblique shock outside the inlet) the drag is increased quite substantially and the pressure recovery drops. This can be improved by adapting the geometry so that the oblique shocks attached to the spike tip are captured by the cowl lip and the system of oblique shocks continues in the inlet and terminates with a weak shock. This is called a **mixed compression inlet** . 

**==> picture [250 x 221] intentionally omitted <==**

**==> picture [252 x 153] intentionally omitted <==**

To summarize the total pressure recovery for different inlets: 

**==> picture [295 x 187] intentionally omitted <==**

54 

## **Lecture 7** 

## **Engine exhaust** 

The main purpose of the nozzle is to convert the potential energy that is available after the turbine into kinetic energy (and then thrust) at a minimum pressure loss. Ideally the exit pressure at the nozzle is equal to the ambient pressure to reduce losses in the generation of thrust (if this is true all of the potential energy that could be converted into kinetic energy and then thrust has been converted). When this occurs we talk about **complete expansion** , however this is typically not the case. Another design objective of the nozzle for civil aviation is to suppress jet noise. 

For most engines the thrust reverser system need to be considered. This is a part of the exhaust system, which has to be designed with this in mind. 

Note: for military aircrafts the exhaust system has some additional requirements such as: permit afterburner operations, allow for thrust vectoring, minimize radar cross section and permit the control of the engine operating characteristics (by changing the nozzle area the mass flow rate can be changed. Only for military a/c). For some low bypass ratio engines the core and bypass flow need to be mixed in the nozzle before expansion, therefore this is in some cases a requirement for the nozzle. 

All of these requirements have to be met against minimum cost, weight and drag while meeting the reliability goals. 

Nozzles can be classified as follows: 

- **Convergent** : this is the most common type of exhaust system used for most civil engines. Typically at design condition in the converging section, also known as the throat, the flow is sonic. This geometry accelerates the gasses converting potential energy into kinetic. 

- **Convergent-Divergent** : the convergent section is followed by a divergent nozzle. This type of nozzle is used for military aircrafts and rocket propulsion. The flow reaches sonic conditions at the throat and keeps expanding in the divergent section reaching even higher supersonic velocities. 

- **Ejector** : used for supersonic aircrafts. This kind of nozzle entrains ambient air in order to reduce the jet velocity and therefore the jet noise. 

- **Plug** : not used anymore. This is a kind of convergent-divergent nozzle that allows for the variation of the geometry. 

- **Single Expansion Ramp Nozzle SERN** : used only for hypersonic aircrafts. The upper surface of the nozzle is actually the body of the aircraft. The geometry is designed so that the expansion continues outside the nozzle and onto the body. 

**==> picture [347 x 189] intentionally omitted <==**

For commercial engines an unmixed convergent nozzle is typically used. The bypass and core have separate nozzles that allow for separate expansion. As we have already said, on some older jets or business jets, the two flows are actually mixed before the expansion. 

55 

**==> picture [318 x 169] intentionally omitted <==**

From a thermodynamic point of view mixing lowers the overall exhaust velocity for the same thrust, improving propulsive efficiency (kinetic energy losses are reduced) and reducing noise. However, mixing incurs pressure losses, reducing total pressure available at the nozzle and slightly lowering ideal thrust. Also the mixer itself is heavy and requires space. 

To take into account all the losses that occur in a real nozzle we can introduce the **isentropic nozzle efficiency** , which is given as the ratio between the enthalpy jump of the real expansion (between the total pressure and the ambient static pressure) and the ideal isentropic expansion: 

**==> picture [293 x 163] intentionally omitted <==**

Since no work or heat is begin added to the flow, the total temperature stays constant, while the total pressure slightly decreases due to friction with the nozzle walls. The sources of nozzle losses are: 

- **Exhaust velocity angularity** : due to the swirl introduced in the turbine or the nozzle itself the flow is not perfectly axial and this component cannot contribute to thrust generation. This is considered a loss. 

- **Flow leakage** : relevant only for variable geometry nozzles. These nozzles are made up of petals that overlap with each other and airflow can leak through the gaps leading to losses. 

- **Friction** inside the boundary layer. 

In engineering practice the isentropic efficiency is hardly ever used, instead the following coefficients are defined to account for losses: 

- **Discharge coefficient** : given as the ratio of the real mass flow rate and the ideal one. Can be expressed as the ratio between the effective area of the nozzle and the geometric area 

- **Velocity coefficient** : ratio between the real jet velocity and the ideal one 

- **Thrust coefficient** : given as the product of the two previous ones. This is equal to the ratio between the actual thrust generated by the nozzle and the ideal one. 

Typical values for these coefficient are between 0.95-0.98. 

56 

To determine whether or not a nozzle is chocked we have to look at the pressure ratio across the whole nozzle. If the pressure ratio Pt7/P0 is over the **critical pressure ratio** then the nozzle is choked. The critical pressure ratio can be intuitively defined by looking at a tank with a small nozzle. The flow through the nozzle is determined by the ratio between the pressure outside and inside the tank. As this ratio increases (either the pressure inside increases or the pressure outside decreases) the mass flow through the nozzle increases up until the flow reaches sonic conditions at the nozzle throat. At this point information about pressure differences cannot travel upstream since it travels at the speed of sound, meaning that any increase in the pressure ratio does not correspond to a change in mass flow rate. The only thing that changes is that the pressure of the flow at the nozzle exit is different from the outside pressure and the expansion continues outside. The expansion is **incomplete** . 

**==> picture [306 x 192] intentionally omitted <==**

The equations above are used to evaluate the critical temperature and pressure ratios in case of an isentropic expansion. Depending on the nozzle efficiency it can increase slightly since higher pressure ratios are needed to reach sonic conditions. 

Here are some examples of calculation for an unchoked nozzle with complete expansion: 

**==> picture [237 x 139] intentionally omitted <==**

**==> picture [216 x 128] intentionally omitted <==**

For a choked nozzle with incomplete expansion: 

57 

**==> picture [313 x 151] intentionally omitted <==**

For choked nozzles if the pressure ratio continues to increase we see that we start getting losses of thrust that are accounted by the thrust coefficient. This is due to the fact that the expansion that occurs outside of the nozzle does not contribute to thrust generation. Some of this energy is still recuperated from the pressure thrust term, however it is not as efficient as directly converting that energy into kinetic energy. 

**==> picture [333 x 141] intentionally omitted <==**

Let’s now look at how the flow behaves in a convergent divergent nozzle: 

**==> picture [239 x 245] intentionally omitted <==**

When the pressure ratio across the nozzle is 1 there is no pressure gradient to drive the flow and the mass flow rate is zero. Once we start rising the pressure ratio the flow starts flowing through the nozzle, until it reaches a maximum velocity at the throat and slows down again the divergent part. This situation persists for all pressure ratios under the critical pressure ratio, with increasing velocities at the throat and exit as the pressure ratio increases. At the critical pressure ratio (or slightly below it), the flow reaches sonic conditions at the throat and then slows down again in the divergent part. 

58 

For higher pressure ratios there is one design pressure ratio for which the expansion is complete. In this situation the flow reaches the throat at sonic conditions and keeps expanding in the divergent part. In this case we have that the pressure at the exit of the nozzle is equal to the ambient pressure. For every pressure ratio between the critical one and PE however we have that a normal shock occurs in the nozzle. The flow keeps expanding in the divergent part of the nozzle until it cannot manage the pressure difference between the flow itself and the ambient pressure. Therefore a rapid compression occurs in the nozzle that brings the flow to subsonic conditions. After that the flow slows down further in the divergent part (since it’s subsonic) and the pressure rises to the ambient one). This condition has to be avoided at all cost since the normal shock introduces a lot of noise, structural damages and losses. 

In this region, as the pressure ratios keep increasing the normal shock moves downstream, until we reach PE, at which point the normal shock is on the exit section of the nozzle. For pressure ratios in between the ideal pressure ratio PF and PE we have that the flow will follow the ideal expansion curve and reach the nozzle exit with a pressure that is lower than the ambient pressure. This is why, for this and the previous regime the nozzle is said to be **overexpanded** . The compression of the flow occurs outside of the nozzle through a system of oblique shocks. 

Finally if the pressure ratio is higher than the ideal pressure ratio the nozzle is **underexpanded** , since the flow will still follow the ideal expansion curve and have a higher pressure than the ambient one. Therefore, the flow will continue to expand outside the nozzle through expansion fans which flare the plume. This is then followed by the formation of shock diamonds ( **Mach disks** ) further downstream to realign the flow. 

**==> picture [327 x 170] intentionally omitted <==**

If we look in detail the sequence of compression and expansion waves for an over and under expanded nozzle we see: 

**==> picture [243 x 174] intentionally omitted <==**

The main difference is that for an over expanded nozzle the first thing that occurs outside the nozzle are oblique shocks that attach to the nozzle lip and converge into a Mach disk. This is then followed by a succession of expansion and compression fans. When compression fans converge a Mach disk is formed. 

59 

For an under expanded nozzle the first thing the flow encounters are expansion fans, which get reflected in an unlike sense as compression waves on the exhaust plume boundary and coalesce into oblique shocks and Mach disks. 

From the graph below we can see that convergent nozzles have better thrust coefficients at low pressure ratios (or lower flight Mach number. Typically until M ~1.5). However, their efficiency drops once the pressure ratios surpass pressure ratios that are a bit higher than the critical pressure ratio (pressure ratios of around 5). That is supersonic aircrafts and fighters use convergent-divergent nozzles. 

**==> picture [235 x 150] intentionally omitted <==**

We can see that as the isentropic efficiency of the nozzle increases the pressure ratio after which the C-D nozzle becomes better gets lower and lower. This is because as the isentropic efficiency increases the pressure ratio required for the flow to become supersonic decreases. 

Note: a variable area nozzle, as we have already said, can be used to control the mass flow rate through the engine. We can see this from the compressor map: 

**==> picture [212 x 184] intentionally omitted <==**

By opening the area of the nozzle the mass flow rate through the engine is increased and therefore the operating line moves away from the surge line, increasing the operational margin. 

## **Thrust reversers** 

Thrust reversers are used to reduce break wear during landing and add a layer of safety especially for wet and icy runways. They cannot be used for the certification of the landing distance in dry conditions due to their low reliability. 

The basic idea is do redirect the exhaust gasses forward in order to produce negative thrust. To do this there are three different types of mechanisms: 

60 

- **Bucket type reversers** : two panels close over the exhaust and reverse both the core and bypass flows. Not used anymore if not only for buisness jets. 

- **Cascade type reversers** : the nozzle cowl shifts backward and the engine nacelle opens up, revealing cascade grids which redirect the gasses forward. Blocker doors block the flow from going out the nozzle. 

- **Petal type** : doors open all over the nacelle. 

**==> picture [209 x 117] intentionally omitted <==**

## **Thrust vectoring** 

Thrust vectoring means that the axis along which the thrust is applied is changed. This is only used for military aircrafts and can be used for short take-off or even vertical take-off. 

**==> picture [208 x 105] intentionally omitted <==**

**Lecture 8** 

## **Combustion chamber** 

The combustion chamber is located between the compressor and turbine in a gas turbine and is required to convert the chemical energy of the fuel into thermal energy with the smallest possible pressure loss and with the least emission of undesirable chemicals. In other words, the combustor provides the heat input into the gas turbine cycle. It receives air from the compressor, introduces a stream of fuel into it, creates the conditions for the fuel and air to mix and react and, eventually, delivers a mixture of hot post-combustion gases to the turbine. Such a process is commonly referred to as **internal combustion** . 

For the sake of analysis, combustion systems are generally classified as either those of constant pressure or constant volume. 

One of the first combustors for a gas turbine engine was the whittle combustor: 

**==> picture [257 x 128] intentionally omitted <==**

61 

For the first combustors the only objective was to introduce heat into the cycle, without having to worry about emissions. Nowadays the combustion chamber design is not only focused on burning the fuel efficiently but also introducing the minimum amount of pollutants as possible. 

Combustion chambers can be classified depending on: 

- Direction of the flow: 

   - **Reverse** : the Whittle combustor is a reverse flow combustion chamber, where the flow comes in from one end, heat is added and the flow then reverses its direction and goes out the same way. This is done to achieve a satisfactory residence time. Typical of old combustion chambers. 

   - **Straight** : flow comes in one end, heat is added in the middle and then exhaust gasses come out the other end. 

- Combustor type: the geometry of the combustor 

   - **Annular** : the combustion chamber shape is cylindrical. This type is the most used in modern engines. 

   - **Can of tubular** combustors: used in the first generations of engines. They are made up of smaller cans places in a circle. 

   - **Can-annular** combustor: mix of the two previous solutions. 

**==> picture [352 x 197] intentionally omitted <==**

The early gas turbine engines used can type combustors, which had holes in the sides to connect all of the cans that were positioned in a circle. This was to equalize the pressure in all of the cans in case of an issue or to reignite one of the flames. 

In the 70’s the design shifted towards an annular architecture. This has the advantage that flame propagation is much easier and less cooling air is required due to the reduced surface area of the combustor. The **liner** (material that “lines” the combustion chamber walls) has holes that allow air unused in the combustion to form a thin film which takes away heat from the liner allowing temperatures that are higher than the melting point of the material. 

## **Enthalpy** 

Enthalpy is one of the most used properties to study the process of combustion. There are three types of enthalpy we can use: 

- **Sensible** : energy related to temperature increase w.r.t. a reference temperature 

- **Latent** : energy required for phase transformation 

- **Reaction enthalpy** : energy that is obtained from an exothermic chemical process or energy required for an endothermic process. For the study of combustion processes, reaction enthalpy is the property of interest. 

62 

Note: typically sensible enthalpy is lower than latent enthalpy, which is itself lower than the reaction enthalpy. 

A type of reaction enthalpy is the **enthalpy of formation** , which is the amount of energy released at a given temperature when 1 mole of an element is produced (for reference elements like H2, O2 and N2 the formation enthalpy is zero). This can be used to evaluate the amount of heat released from a chemical reaction. 

On a molecular level, as we go from elemental molecules to gaseous atoms we need to give an amount of heat that is equal to the **energy of atomization** (which is equal to the bond energy). The jump from the enthalpy of reactant molecules and products molecules is defined as the enthalpy of reaction. This can be positive or negative depending on if the reaction is exothermic or endothermic. The jump from the elemental molecules and the reactant molecules is quantified by the **enthalpy of formation** of the reactants. The same is true for the products. The **dissociation energy** is instead defined as the energy jump from the reactants or products and their respective gaseous atoms. 

**==> picture [306 x 160] intentionally omitted <==**

If we chart the total enthalpy of both the products and the reactants we see that they both increase with temperature (sensible enthalpy increases). If we were to have an adiabatic combustion, no energy would leave the system, therefore the total enthalpy would remain constant (formation enthalpy is transformed into sensible enthalpy). The new thermodynamic state would be on the reactants curve at a higher temperature. 

**==> picture [213 x 145] intentionally omitted <==**

The **total** (or **absolute** ) **enthalpy** at a given temperature can be defined as the sum of the formation enthalpy and the sensible enthalpy. 

**==> picture [207 x 94] intentionally omitted <==**

63 

If we consider a process of combustion involving methane and oxygen we would get CO2 and water vapor. The **heat of reaction** (which is just the reaction enthalpy of a combustion process) of this process is just the difference in formation enthalpy of the products and the reactants. 

**==> picture [148 x 35] intentionally omitted <==**

**==> picture [227 x 69] intentionally omitted <==**

From this the heating value of a fuel can be evaluated as the ratio between the heat of reaction and the molar mass of the fuel. We can define two types of fuel calorific values (amount of heat released by combustion of one unit mass of fuel) the **Lower Heating Value** LHV and the **Higher Heating Value** HHV. The first is defined as the amount of heat released by combustion of 1kg of fuel, where all the water in the products is in gaseous form (has absorbed part of the heat released by the reaction to change phase), while the HHV is defined as the amount of heat released by combustion of 1kg of fuel, where all the water in the products is in liquid form. 

## **Bond energy** 

We’re considering the transformation of chemical energy into heat. Chemical energy is the energy that is conserved in chemical bonds: when a bond is broken energy is released and vice versa. 

**==> picture [399 x 165] intentionally omitted <==**

The bond energy is defined as the energy required to separate two atoms, in other words the minimum potential energy relative to when the atoms are infinitely separated. The minima coincides with the equilibrium state. To move away from this equilibrium, energy needs to be provided; this is the bond energy. 

**==> picture [237 x 166] intentionally omitted <==**

64 

If a isobaric or isochoric reaction is carried out in stages, the algebraic sum of the heat evolved in separate stages is equal to the total amount of heat released when the reaction occurs directly, which means that the heat liberated in a reaction is independent of the path of the reaction between the initial and the final states. This is known as the **Hess Law** . 

Let’s consider the oxidation of methane (combustion). The simple reaction is: 

**==> picture [147 x 17] intentionally omitted <==**

However, in reality there are approximately 50 species involved and more than 300 reactions occur. Thanks to the Hess law we can still evaluate the enthalpy of the reaction from the initial and final states, independently of the stages in between (since combustion can be assumed to be occurring at constant pressure or at constant volume). 

The fuel used in aviation are all different types of hydrocarbons, specifically alkanes, aromatics (characterized by an aromatic group similar to benzene) and alcohols (characterized by an hydroxyl group). From the following table the heating values and the stochiometric air to fuel ratio of these fuels can be computed. The **stochiometric air to fuel ratio** is the ideal ratio between air and fuel moles to have a complete combustion, where all the reactants are transformed into products, with no leftover reactants. 

**==> picture [351 x 209] intentionally omitted <==**

Some observations: 

- The enthalpies of reaction for many hydrocarbon fuels on mass basis is similar, between 40-50 MJ/kg. 

- The stoichiometric air-fuel ratio for many hydrocarbons lie between 13:1 to 17:1 

- Some fuels have positive formation enthalpies 

- All fuels have negative enthalpies of reaction (they release heat when they participate in a reaction) 

- The enthalpies of reaction for alcohols is less than non-oxygenated fuels (as oxygen does not provide any energy of reaction) 

- Commonly used fossil fuels are a mixture of several hydrocarbon compounds and do not have a precise chemical formulation 

When a mixture of air and fuel burns with a fuel to air ratio lower than the stochiometric ratio, the combustion is called **lean combustion** since there is a lower than ideal quantity of fuel in the mixture and some air will not participate in the reaction and will instead absorb some of the heat released during the reaction. Thus the final temperature of the exhaust gasses will be lower. 

65 

If the mixture of air and fuel burns with a fuel to air ratio higher than the stochiometric ratio, the combustion is called **rich combustion** since there is a higher than ideal quantity of fuel in the mixture and some of this fuel will not be burned during the reaction. 

If a fuel-air mixture burns adiabatically at constant pressure, the absolute enthalpy of the reactants at the initial state equals the absolute enthalpy of the products at the final state. The final-state temperature is defined as the constant-pressure **adiabatic flame temperature** . 

**==> picture [125 x 30] intentionally omitted <==**

The adiabatic flame temperature will be highest when the air to fuel ratio is close to the stochiometric one, since there won’t be any unused reactants absorbing heat. 

**==> picture [192 x 175] intentionally omitted <==**

**==> picture [221 x 168] intentionally omitted <==**

In these graphs the **equivalence ratio** appears. This is just the ratio between the real fuel to air ratio FAR and the stoichiometric fuel to air ratio FAR,stoich. When it’s lower than one, the mixture is lean, while when it’s higher than one the mixture is rich. 

**==> picture [89 x 17] intentionally omitted <==**

**==> picture [67 x 8] intentionally omitted <==**

We see that the peak in adiabatic flame temperature is slightly shifted to the right of the equivalence ratio of 1. This shift is due mainly to **dissociation** which occurs at high temperatures. From the second graph we see that the CO2 and H2O are the main products of combustion at an equivalence ratio of 1. Since the temperature is so high, many of these products will dissociate absorbing energy and releasing oxygen. At a slightly richer equivalence ratios the equilibrium shifts towards less dissociation, leading to higher temperatures. Also the excess oxygen released from dissociation can be consumed by the extra fuel, leading to a slightly higher temperature. 

66 

Let’s now look at how the pressure in the combustor effect the adiabatic flame temperature. 

**==> picture [215 x 159] intentionally omitted <==**

We see that the adiabatic flame temperature increases with pressure until a plateau is reached. Higher pressure increases the “speed” of the chemical reactions, so it shifts chemical equilibrium toward more complete combustion (for the same residence time) and more dissociation suppression, so less energy is spent breaking molecules apart. This raises the flame temperature. As pressure continues to increase, dissociation becomes minimal and the reactants/products are already near their equilibrium limits, so further pressure increases add little change, producing a plateau. 

**==> picture [256 x 190] intentionally omitted <==**

If we instead consider the effects of the inlet temperature we see that as the inlet temperature increases, the adiabatic flame temperature increases but not 1 to 1. 

**==> picture [263 x 199] intentionally omitted <==**

67 

This is because part of the added inlet energy is consumed by greater product dissociation at the higher flame temperature. Dissociation absorbs energy, reducing how much of the inlet-temperature rise shows up as temperature gain in the adiabatic flame. 

Combustion can be classified in different ways: 

- Based on the mixing of fuel and the oxidizer (main distinction): 

   - **Pre-mixed** : fuel and oxidizer are mixed before the reaction. This solution burns at a selected equivalence ratio and are therefore prone to **flashback** , other instabilities and is hard to ignite. Since the temperatures reached for this kind of combustion are not too high, the NOx emissions are also contained as well as soot production. CO production becomes hard to control. 

   - **Non pre-mixed** : also called diffusion burning (occurs naturally at an equivalence ratio of 1 on the boundary of the flame). It is flashback-free and also very stable. They allow for a broad operating envelope and have reliable ignition. However, these flames produce high amounts of NOx and soot due to the high temperature gradients that form on the boundary between the fuel and the air. Here dissociation of nitrogen and oxygen occur, creating different types of nitrogen oxides. Since the NOx emissions are high, the CO emissions are easy to control 

   - **Partially pre-mixed** : most combustor are of this type. Only a portion of the oxidizer is mixed with the fuel. 

**==> picture [335 x 211] intentionally omitted <==**

- Nature of flow: 

   - **Laminar** 

   - **Turbulent** 

- Phase of reactants: 

   - **Homogeneous** : the reactants are all in the same phase 

   - **Non homogeneous** : the reactants are in different phases such as kerosene (liquid) and air (gas). This requires apposite fuel injectors. 

- Temporal variation: 

   - **Steady** : typical of laminar flow flames 

   - **Non-steady** : typical of turbulent flows 

- Speed of the combustion: 

   - **Deflagration** : subsonic flame front propagation. It is also typically characterized by a decrease in pressure. 

   - **Detonation** : supersonic flame front propagation. Characterized by a large pressure increase 

- Speed of the flow: 

   - **Compressible** 

   - **Non compressible** 

68 

- Heat transfer: 

   - **Adiabatic** : gas turbine combustion is adiabatic since heat is not being extracted from the system, but only used to increase the temperature of the products 

   - **Non adiabatic** : typical for industrial combustion chambers (e.g. furnaces) 

- Convection: 

   - **Natural** 

   - **Forced** 

- Gravity: 

   - **Micro-gravity** : this concerns only rocket propulsion 

   - **Normal gravity** 

- Spatial dependence (strongly dependent on the geometry of the combustor): `o` **1-D flame** 

   - **2-D flame** 

   - **3-D flame** 

- Reaction rate: 

   - **Equilibrium** 

   - **Finite rate** 

## **Combustor components** 

The design of an aero engine combustor is subject to many design requirements. 

**==> picture [237 x 199] intentionally omitted <==**

Note: **pattern factor** is determined by the temperature profile at the combustor exit. A good pattern factor is required for longevity of the first stages of the turbine. 

The main components of a combustor are: diffuser, casing, liner, fuel injector, some type of a flame stabilization device and a cooling arrangement. 

**==> picture [365 x 175] intentionally omitted <==**

69 

Quantitatively, the drop in total pressure associated with combustion is proportional to the dynamic pressure of the flow. Therefore, the compressor discharge velocity should be reduced to minimize the loss. It is customary to do so by incorporating a diffuser at the combustor inlet. Additional functions of the diffuser are to recover the flow dynamic pressure by raising its static pressure and smoothen flow instabilities. 

There are two main design philosophies for the diffuser. One is to employ a relatively **long duct diffuser** to achieve a gradual flow deceleration. The other is the so-called “ **dump** ” **diffuser** , which consists of a short smooth pre-diffuser where the air velocity is reduced to about half its inlet value. At exit, the air is literally dumped into the combustor casing. 

**==> picture [385 x 132] intentionally omitted <==**

The aerodynamic diffuser has smaller pressure losses but results in a considerable length. The dump diffuser is exactly opposite. Its shorter length made this type the automatic design choice for modern aircraft engines. 

Next are the **casing** (supports the pressure load) and **liner** (supports the thermal load). The simplest possible form of combustor would be a straight-walled duct connecting the compressor to the turbine. Unfortunately, this arrangement is impractical due to the fact the pressure loss incurred would be excessive. Simply fitting a diffuser, however, is not sufficient to obtain a viable combustion system. First of all, the air velocity remains too high for a flame to sit. Secondly, pouring fuel directly into the available airflow would result in no flame, as the air to-fuel ratios are too high for the mixture to ignite. The airflow should therefore be partitioned and the flame stabilized. Arrangements for that are provided inside the liner located inside the casing and downstream of the diffuser. The space between the liner and casing is called **annulus** . 

**==> picture [300 x 148] intentionally omitted <==**

The liner is divided into divided into a **primary zone** (PZ), **secondary zone** (SZ) and **dilution zone** (DZ). 

A **fuel injector** and a **flame stabilization device** mark the front boundary of the primary zone. In some cases the PZ may also be preceded by an additional mixture preparation device: a **mixing chamber** (for pre-mixed combustion). The main function of the PZ is to anchor the flame and provide sufficient time, temperature and mixing to achieve complete combustion of the incoming fuel air mixture. If the PZ temperature is around or above 2000 K, dissociation reactions will result in significant amounts of CO in the gases. If the residence time of the fuel-air mixture in the PZ is too short, appreciable amounts of UHC and CO will be present due to incomplete combustion. These products are major polluting species and also absorb a lot of the heat released. 

70 

To provide additional time and space for the burnout of CO and UHC, the secondary zone succeeds the PZ. The right conditions are created in the SZ by adding extra air to reduce the temperature, while not quenching the combustion. At low altitude operation (higher pressure), the SZ needs to recover dissociation losses and complete the burning of any leftovers from the PZ. Some of the soot that is produced in the PZ is also oxidized in the SZ. 

At high altitude operation (lower pressure) the reaction rate in the PZ is lower due to the lower pressure and so the SZ needs to act as an extension to the PZ allowing for higher residence time. The remaining air is admitted into the dilution zone, mixing it with the core air. This is done to reduce the temperature of the outlet stream and make its temperature pattern acceptable to the turbine. 

Another important element is the fuel injector. Fuel can be in gaseous form or in liquid form. A common method of supplying a gaseous fuel into combustion chamber is forcing it through a specially designed orifice. For liquid fuels it is more complicated since the fuel needs to be vaporized through atomization, otherwise the combustion will occur heterogeneously around the big droplets of fuel. The fuel injectors used for liquid fuels like kerosene are therefore called **atomizers** . 

Air leaves the compressor at a speed on the order of 150 to 200 [m/s] for the case of an industrial gas turbine or a large aircraft engine. The velocity is reduced about twice in the combustor diffuser. It is further decelerated down to 25 to 35 [m/s] in the primary zone of the liner. However the flow velocity still remains far greater than the speed of the flame. To prevent the flame from being blown away, it is therefore necessary to set up local regions with much smaller velocity. The most common solution is to generate a flow reversal in the PZ, which can be obtained by imparting swirl to the flow. Recirculation is created in the core region of the swirl where the amount of rotation is high. 

**==> picture [313 x 166] intentionally omitted <==**

Somewhere near that boundary, the velocity assumes the value that matches the value of the flame speed. This is the region where the flame “anchors”. **Swirlers** also contribute to fuel-air mixing and provide effective ways to control the stability and intensity of combustion. Other ways to supply flame stabilization are: 

**==> picture [316 x 86] intentionally omitted <==**

**==> picture [305 x 73] intentionally omitted <==**

71 

The stabilization by swirling flow assisted by secondary flow injection is the most common solution and it is a mix of the two previous solutions. 

The combustor liner is exposed to very high temperatures during gas turbine operation. To ensure the life of the liner, it is necessary to remove the heat transferred to it and prevents contact with the hot combustion gases. An efficient way to do this is **film cooling** . This technique employs holes through which air is injected in the axial direction along the inner surface of the liner wall to provide a protective cooling film. This film is being gradually destroyed downstream. Therefore, normal practice is to provide a succession of cooling-hole bands along the length of the liner. 

In summary, the incoming airflow is split into two flows since there is too much air for a complete combustion; 20-25% of the air goes into the core while the rest flow in the annulus . The core air then enters the diffuser where it is slowed down. Fuel is then injected into the core flow and the swirler aid with mixing. This all occurs in the primary zone. The flame stay in between the primary and intermediate zone. Meanwhile the liner walls are cooled by the secondary flow that enters through small holes and forms a protective film. 

Then in the dilution zone the rest of the air is mixed to ensure the correct temperature profile to the turbine inlet. 

**==> picture [246 x 125] intentionally omitted <==**

The evolution of combustion and the temperature variation in a typical combustion chamber are plotted in the graphs below. 

**==> picture [197 x 143] intentionally omitted <==**

**==> picture [196 x 149] intentionally omitted <==**

Most of the temperature rise occurs in the PZ, since that’s where the flame sits. Then in the SZ and the DZ temperature decreases again since secondary flow is being mixed with the core flow. 

72 

## **Flammability limits** 

Gas turbine combustion is supposed to be a self-sustaining continuous process under broad operating conditions. We can define a region of equivalence ratio and air mass flow (or air velocity) outside of which combustion cannot take place. 

**==> picture [184 x 198] intentionally omitted <==**

We see that as the flow velocity increases the equivalence ratio range over which combustion can occur decreases. The curve is defined by a **rich extinction limit** and a **lean extinction limit** . However, a combustor must operate well within these limits, since there are many effects we want to avoid close to the boundaries. 

**==> picture [234 x 190] intentionally omitted <==**

**Flashback** is a phenomena in which the flame propagates upstream and can only occur at low velocity flow. This can greatly damage the combustor. Near the limits high frequency oscillations in the flame occur, which can cause a flame-out. These acoustic oscillations then lead to unsteady heat release, which in turn causes flow perturbation that cause acoustic oscillations. This cycle continues and it creates pressure fluctuations which decrease the combustors efficiency and structural vibrations. This is what we refer to with **combustor instability** . 

Just like for the adiabatic flame temperature, inlet temperature and pressure have an effect on these limits. As we increase these two properties the limits move to the right, increasing the possible operating range of the combustor. 

Higher pressure and higher inlet temperature both make ignition and reaction kinetics faster and easier, so mixtures that were previously too lean or too rich can now sustain a flame. This is particularly advantageous during take-off, when the pressure is much higher. The critical condition is relight at cruise, since pressure and temperature are both very low. 

73 

**==> picture [155 x 180] intentionally omitted <==**

**==> picture [149 x 179] intentionally omitted <==**

If we look at the below chart for example we see that engines tend to have overall equivalence ratios that are outside of these limits, especially at take-off. Therefore the design of the combustion chamber needs to endure higher local equivalence ratios so that we stay in the flammability limits. Also bigger engines have higher overall equivalence ratios since they operate at higher altitudes. 

**==> picture [286 x 116] intentionally omitted <==**

The stability of the flame is also a function of other combustion parameters such as: 

- **Ignition delay** : the time between when a fuel–air mixture is exposed to ignition conditions and when rapid heat release (combustion) actually begins. The delay increases with inlet temperature. 

- **Activation energy** : the minimum energy the reactants must acquire for a chemical reaction to proceed at an appreciable rate. As the equivalence ratio increases and approaches 1, the activation energy decreases. Varies depending on the type of fuels. 

## **Combustor efficiency** 

Since the purpose of the combustor is to provide heat to the flow, the combustor efficiency can be defined as the ratio between the actual enthalpy rise and the ideal enthalpy rise, where the enthalpy rise is equal to the heat added to the system since the combustion is isobaric. 

**==> picture [166 x 50] intentionally omitted <==**

Typically combustor efficiencies are very high and tend to increase with pressure and temperature due to the increased reaction rate (for the same residency time, combustion is more complete). The opposite is true for the Mach number, since this decreases residency time. 

Since the design point of the combustor is the cruise condition we see that the efficiency tends to increase as we increase the thrust generated. 

74 

**==> picture [205 x 163] intentionally omitted <==**

The main losses in the combustor come from the pressure losses that occur due to friction, mixing, and heat addition as well as dissociation that occurs at high temperatures. Other factors are incomplete combustion and heat losses. 

Pressure loss due friction is a major contributor especially in the DZ since the flow is accelerating back up again. 

Let’s now summarize all of the characteristics of an actual aero engine combustor: 

- Primarily turbulent combustion since the velocity of the flow is still very high even though the flow is slowed down significantly 

- External ignition is required to light the combustion, then combustion is self-sustained unlike in automotive engines 

- Constant pressure distribution (very small pressure losses) 

- Pressure losses penalize performance and have to be limited 

- Low relative volume and weight 

- Fast volume and heat dynamics 

- Low residence time (in the order of a couple of milliseconds)) 

- Adiabatic combustion (only very small heat losses that penalize the performance) 

- High pressure and inlet temperatures (40 (large engines)-60 (smaller engines) bar and T>900K) 

Note: gas turbines for power generation operate at lower pressures (<30bar) and lower temperatures (<750K), while micro turbines operate at very low pressures (3-5 bar) and quite high temperatures (T~1000K). 

As fuels the aviation industry mainly uses liquid kerosene although different fuels are being studied both in liquid form and gaseous form. 

We also observe that there are different types of combustor types: 

- Conventional 

- RQL, lean burn, staged combustor, etc... 

- Alternative technologies 

- Use of catalysts and inhibitors (not currently used, active area of research) 

## **Fuel** 

The fuel used by aviation is kerosene, which is a product of the distillation of crude oil together with gasoline, diesel and many other petrol derivatives. Kerosene is a mix of tens of different compounds, whose relative concentration vary depending on the use cases of the fuel. The compounds with the highest concentration are **alkanes** (paraffines) which have the following chemical formula: 

CnH2n+2 

75 

**Aromatics** are also present with very small concentrations to reduce the soot formation in the combustion chamber. Many other chemical species are present and their concentrations are specifically determined to obtain the specified requirements. The most common carbon chains present in kerosene are the C-11 and C-10/C-12 chains as we can see from this distribution. 

**==> picture [253 x 143] intentionally omitted <==**

## **Engine emissions** 

Engine emissions are strictly regulated for the Landing and Take-off cycle LTO. This cycle is made up of 4 phases: the approach, the taxi, the take-off and the climb. The cruise condition is not regulated. This is mainly to reduce local air pollution near airports. All of the products of the combustion, such as carbon monoxide, nitrogen oxides, smoke and unburned reactants are highly harmful for human beings. 

The component of the emissions that is most regulated are the NOx emissions since they can have the most dangerous effects. 

There are many different ways that nitrogen oxides can form, however we focus on **thermal NOx** , which is created inside the combustion chamber especially at high temperatures. 

**==> picture [188 x 159] intentionally omitted <==**

Factors that influence the NOx emissions are all the factors that influence the adiabatic flame temperature, since the emissions increase exponentially after a certain temperature is surpassed. Also residence time plays a role (higher residence time corresponds to higher NOx emissions, since the reaction shifts towards equilibrium and a more complete combustion). 

**==> picture [203 x 144] intentionally omitted <==**

76 

Even though the peak of the adiabatic flame temperature is slightly on the rich side, we see that peak emissions are slightly on the lean side. This is because on the rich side the availability of oxygen is less. We can see that different temperatures in the combustor are reached for different phases of the mission: 

**==> picture [183 x 193] intentionally omitted <==**

The CO production is opposite of the NOx production: it is highest for idle conditions and lowest for take-off and climb. 

**==> picture [173 x 186] intentionally omitted <==**

Soot emissions seem to follow the same trend as NOx emissions. The controlling factors are the fuel to air ratio, pressure, temperature, quality of fuel injection and mode of the fuel injection. The equivalence ratio is particularly important for the soot emissions (soot is formed mainly in rich conditions), therefore the injection quality and mode are important. 

**==> picture [171 x 192] intentionally omitted <==**

77 

**==> picture [362 x 174] intentionally omitted <==**

The main emissions from aero engines are molecular nitrogen and molecular oxygen since the combustion typically occurs in lean conditions, some of the air does not participate in the combustion process. 

For higher thrust settings the amount of CO2, H2O, NOx and soot produced are higher since more fuel is being burned, while the oxygen concentration decreases since it’s being used up. For the CO2 and H2O it’s because the amount produced per kg of fuel burned stays pretty much constant. CO emissions follow the opposite trend since it is formed at lower temperatures (it cannot be oxidized into CO2) and is also a product of incomplete combustion (at lower temperatures the reaction is further from being a complete one). 

Unburned hydrocarbons are highest when the thrust is minimum. 

Note: UHC and CO behave similarly, as do NOx and soot emissions. 

By superimposing all of these trends we get a pretty small low emissions window around low equivalence ratios. That is why all of modern engines have an overall lean equivalence ratio. 

**==> picture [178 x 150] intentionally omitted <==**

## **RQL** 

**Rich burn-Quick quench-Lean burn** engines are a combustor architecture used to reduce the NOx emissions. 

**==> picture [210 x 115] intentionally omitted <==**

78 

The equivalence ratio is kept quite rich in the PZ (close to 2), which leads to relatively low temperatures in the PZ. This is followed by a quench zone where a lot of air is added from outside the liner, leading to a last zone of lean burn. This way the region where NOx emission are very high is avoided by avoiding the very high temperatures that occur at stoichiometric FARs, without sacrificing too much the efficiency. In the figure below we can see that NOx emissions (in black) are very low at high and low equivalence ratios. The quench process occurs so rapidly that the existence of nearstoichiometric solutions is kept to a minimum, therefore the temperature does not surpass the threshold for NOx generation. 

**==> picture [210 x 169] intentionally omitted <==**

Most of the modern engines use the RQL technology. A big amount of soot is produced in the rich burn zone since the equivalence ratio is high, however it is usually oxidized in the SZ and DZ. 

**==> picture [219 x 161] intentionally omitted <==**

## **TAPS** 

A **Twin Annular Premixing Swirler** is an alternative technology used by GE for their combustors. In this case the primary zone is already lean since almost 70% of the incoming airflow goes in the core of the combustor. This is a huge difference compared to the RQL combustor where only 25% of the air actually goes in the core. Since the amount of air for TAPS combustors is very high there is also a need for very complex swirlers to slow down the flow and stabilize the flame. 

Note: we can compare some different characteristics between industrial and aero combustion chambers. 

79 

**==> picture [315 x 178] intentionally omitted <==**

## **Lecture 10 Turbomachinery** 

An aero engine is mainly made up of turbomachinery: both turbines, both compressors and the fan are all examples of turbomachinery. 

Let’s first look at the compressor. There are many different types of compressors and they can be classified into: 

- **Mechanical compressors** : they use moving mechanical parts to compress the air. Can be further classified into: 

   - **Positive displacement** : compression of the fluid is achieved by compressing the fluid in a closed volume that reduces. 

   - **Dynamic** : compression is achieved by doing work on a moving fluid without changing its volume. Can be then classified based on the direction of movement of the fluid: 

      - **Axial** 

      - **Centrifugal** 

      - **Diagonal** : mix of the previous two 

## **Axial compressor** 

The most used architecture for compressors in aero engines are **axial compressors** . In an axial compressor stages are positioned in succession in order to achieve a high-pressure ratio in a gradual way. Each stage is made up of a **rotor** followed by a **stator** . Mechanical power is used to increase the kinetic energy of the fluid, which is then converted in pressure by a diffusion process. Work is done on the fluid by the rotors, which are connected to the shafts that drive them and provide them the power harvested by the turbine stages. The stator (or diffuser) transforms this kinetic energy into an increase in pressure through diffusion. This part is also used to modify the direction of the fluid and make it as it was before entering the next rotor. This way the stages can be repeated. 

**==> picture [293 x 153] intentionally omitted <==**

80 

From the figure above we can also see that the stator blades are also connected to a mechanism, which allows them to rotate around their axis. These are called **variable stator blades** and allow the compressor to function even in the harshest operating conditions without stall. 

Another thing to note is that the size of the blades decreases progressively as we move through the compressor. This is done to maintain the axial velocity constant (to have constant velocity triangles and therefore a constant loading and work distribution of the compressor stages); since pressure is increasing, density is also increasing, thus to maintain the axial velocity, the cross-sectional area needs to decrease given that the mass flow rate is constant. 

In most cases, before the first stage of the compressor, a IGV **Inlet Guide Vane** is present, which impart a swirl component to the axial flow, aligning it so that the relative velocity the first rotor sees is equal to the relative velocity all the other rotors see. 

The root of the blades are made to slide into place around a disk that is connected to the shaft. They are then locked in place through different mechanisms, such as: 

**==> picture [275 x 183] intentionally omitted <==**

For high performance engines, the blades are machined with the disk so to reduce mechanical complexity and weight. These kinds of disks are called **bladed disks** (also called **blisks** ) and are much harder to manufacture, much more expensive and are impossible to repair (they can only be replaced). 

**==> picture [323 x 147] intentionally omitted <==**

Bladed disks are typically never used in civil aircrafts and are only used in some military engines. 

For non-variable stator blades, they are anchored both at the root and at the tip. In some cases to save on weight the stator blades are only connected to the outer shell of the compressor and are only glued to the core. 

For smaller engines sometimes the saving in cost of only anchoring one side of the stator blades are enough to outweigh the increase in losses due to the presence of a gap. 

81 

**==> picture [223 x 133] intentionally omitted <==**

For the connection between the rotor blades and the shaft there are some different solutions: 

- **Disk rotor** : generally used only for older engines. A series of individual disks connected together connect the blades of the rotor stages to the shaft. They are easy simple and easy to manufacture but they have poor stress distribution. 

- **Drum rotor** : a hollow cylindrical drum is mounted on the shaft and on its surface all of the rotor blades are mounted. The stator blades are mounted on the outer casing and have a certain tip clearance with the drum’s surface. They have a better centrifugal stress distribution, but can be heavier than disks. 

- **Drum rotor with variable vanes** : in this case the cylindrical surface has indents which allow the presence of the mechanism which holds the VSVs. 

**==> picture [219 x 201] intentionally omitted <==**

The design of these disks are very important since they are subject to very high centrifugal stresses and can harmfully contribute to the propagation of vibrations that can lead to structural damage. Unlike the compressor blades, the fan blades are hollow inside and have an internal honeycomb structure to reduce weight. Moreover, fan blades typically have airfoil sections and supports towards the tip that allow them to rest on one another, greatly reducing vibrations. These supports are typical of older turbofans and are not used anymore in modern turbofans. 

**==> picture [196 x 145] intentionally omitted <==**

82 

Modern fan blades are much more complex than older fan blades. They have more exotic 3D geometries and are usually made of carbon fibre composites with titanium tips. Additionally, they have very low hub-to-tip ratios and so the flow is dominated by 3D effects. For this reason, together with the fact that each section sees completely different angles of attack, is why their 3D geometry is very important. 

Let’s now consider more in depth how an axial compressor functions. For our considerations we assume that there are no 3D effects and that the flow is completely axial. The variation of pressure and velocity along a generic axial compressor is as follows: 

**==> picture [181 x 193] intentionally omitted <==**

Pressure increases gradually, while the absolute velocity increases in the rotor an decreases in the stator. 

We now consider a compressor and a fluid element along its path. 

**==> picture [285 x 172] intentionally omitted <==**

**==> picture [383 x 121] intentionally omitted <==**

Because of the blading arrangements, the flow inside a turbo-machine rotor is unsteady and asymmetric. However, the unsteadiness is periodic so that, on average, we may omit the contribution of the time derivative of the velocity vector to the torque equation. 

83 

**==> picture [407 x 380] intentionally omitted <==**

Euler’s formula is used to evaluate the power needed by a compressor stage. This can be applied to all types of turbomachinery, including centrifugal compressors and turbines. The power can also be expressed as the total enthalpy jump across the compressor stage. We can study a single stage by looking at a cross-section along its mean line: 

**==> picture [218 x 175] intentionally omitted <==**

84 

**==> picture [245 x 274] intentionally omitted <==**

Note the direction of rotation w.r.t. the camber of the airfoils, since work is being done on the flow. To study turbomachinery blades **velocity triangles** are used, which represent the following velocity relation: 

W + U = C 

Where U is the rotational speed of the blade section we’re considering, W is the flow relative velocity to the blade and C is the flow’s absolute velocity. For an axial compressor: 

**==> picture [217 x 132] intentionally omitted <==**

We assume that the angle of the relative velocity of the flow leaving a blade is the same as the angle of the trailing edge of the blade. Also to reduce losses, we assume the stages are designed so that the incoming velocity (relative for the rotor and absolute for the stator) is parallel to the leading edge of the blade. 

To repeat stages the absolute velocity coming out of the stator needs to be equal to the incoming absolute velocity to the rotor. 

Looking at velocity triangles of the rotor and assuming that the axial velocity has the same value throughout, we obtain: 

**==> picture [405 x 42] intentionally omitted <==**

85 

**==> picture [353 x 171] intentionally omitted <==**

So to impart as much power as possible to the fluid to obtain the highest pressure ratio possible, we would like to “turn” the flow as much as possible, however in reality this would lead to enormous losses, so a balance has to be struck. 

From the T-S diagram of a compressor stage we have: 

**==> picture [288 x 170] intentionally omitted <==**

The air is coming in with a velocity V1 and is being compressed by the rotor to a total pressure p0,2, which is slightly lower than the isentropic p0,2,is due to losses (the static pressure is also increasing). We see from the graph that the dynamic pressure at point 2 is also higher than in point 1 since the rotor has also increased the flow velocity. Then from 2-3 the stator increases the static pressure even more by decreasing the dynamic one. The total pressure slightly decreases due to friction losses. Overall, the dynamic pressure of the flow at point 1 should be almost equal to the dynamic pressure at point 3. 

The total temperature increases from 1 to 2 since work is being done on the fluid, while it stays constant between 2 and 3 since no heat or work is exchanged. 

From the evaluation of the power needed by each stage and the isentropic efficiency of the stage, the pressure and temperature ratios can be evaluated. 

**==> picture [275 x 126] intentionally omitted <==**

To achieve high pressure ratios we want to maximize the amount of work a single stage can impart on the fluid. To do this we can increase the rotational velocity u of the rotor, but the centrifugal stresses 

86 

do not allow extremely high rotational velocities; we also would like to have high axial flow velocity, but flow separation and losses limit this term; we would like to have a high tan α2 −tanα1 (which is proportional to how much the rotor blades turn the flow), but there is a limit to the blade curvature caused by flow separation problems. 

Typically diffusion takes place in both rotor and stator, so we can define a **degree of reaction** , which provides a measure of the extent to which the rotor contributes to the overall static pressure rise in the stage. It is normally defined as: 

**==> picture [264 x 155] intentionally omitted <==**

For degree of reaction close to zero, the rotor (called a “ **bucket** ” **rotor** or **impulse rotor** ) is only increasing the velocity of the flow, while all of the diffusion occurs on the stator, vice versa, for degree of reaction of 1 all of the diffusion occurs in the rotor. Typically we want a degree of reaction of around 0.5 for a more gradual pressure increase across the compressor. The higher is the degree of reaction, the more is the amount of energy increased in the rotor and so the higher is the temperature rise in that part of the compressor. Different values of the degree of reaction are responsible for a different shape of the vanes. 

**==> picture [273 x 189] intentionally omitted <==**

All the considerations that have been made refer to an imaginary plane section of the blade. The velocity triangles have been drawn in a tangential plane at the mean radius. At the front end of the compressor the blades are much longer, so there are marked variations in blade speed from root to tip, and it becomes essential to consider 3D effects in analysing the flow. Moreover, in the real condition, the axial velocity changes between root and tip of the blades due of the presence of the boundary layer along the annulus walls. This effect becomes more evident in the later stages, because the boundary layer thickens as the flow progresses, and the area available for the flow is reduced below the geometric area. 

Also the clearance between the blade tips and the blades themselves is constant in absolute value. However, the chord of the later stages is smaller, so the space in between is relatively bigger and tip effects losses are greater. 

87 

**==> picture [257 x 122] intentionally omitted <==**

A so called “ **Work-Done** ” **factor** is introduced to account for the reduction in work capacity caused by the change in axial velocity. Integrating the specific power along the blade height and using the “Work-Done” factor λ, we obtain: 

**==> picture [305 x 168] intentionally omitted <==**

As the number of stages increases the work being done on the fluid is being wasted more and more on losses and increasing the total temperature of the fluid instead of the total pressure. 

## **Centrifugal compressors** 

Centrifugal compressors, unlike axial compressors, change the direction of the incoming axial flow. The flow, which is initially parallel to the axis of rotation, leaves the compressor in a radial manner. The first part of the compression occurs due to work being done on the flow by the compressor blades, however most of the compression actually occurs due to the centrifugal forces that act on the fluid particles when the flow becomes radial. 

**==> picture [184 x 182] intentionally omitted <==**

When the flow comes out of the **impeller** (rotating component) it goes into a **radial diffuser** which slows down the fluid and further increases pressure. 

88 

**==> picture [176 x 166] intentionally omitted <==**

Similarly to an axial compressor, in the rotor the flow’s velocity and pressure are increased thanks to the work being done on the fluid, while in the stator the velocity decreases as the pressure increases. However, the work in this case is mainly being done by centrifugal forces. 

**==> picture [193 x 213] intentionally omitted <==**

The velocity triangles are as follows: 

**==> picture [288 x 169] intentionally omitted <==**

Centrifugal compressors are almost never used in civil aircraft engines but are sometimes used for turboshafts for helicopters or APUs. 

The advantages of the centrifugal compressors is that have very high compression ratios for a single stage. Typical pressure ratios for modern centrifugal compressors are around 7-8 for one stage. 

89 

Therefore, if the required compression ratio is low, radial compressors are lighter and smaller than axial compressors, however after a certain compression ratio axial compressors become much more efficient from a space and weight point of view.  Radial compressors also have the issue that they have a much larger frontal area, while axial compressors can achieve very high pressure ratios without ever changing their frontal area. 

**==> picture [295 x 206] intentionally omitted <==**

Note: **compressor bleed air** can be up to 20% of the total mass flow rate through the engine. This bleed air can be used for many functions like: cabin environmental control, tip clearance control, thrust balancing, etc. One of the main functions of the compressor bleed air is to pressurize the cavities in that are present in the engine. This is done so that the oil present in the shaft, which is at extremely high temperatures, does not get inside the engine and stays inside the shaft. Since it’s always at a high temperature it can evaporate and the mist produced is highly inflammable. This mist is typically expelled at the back of the engine. 

**==> picture [244 x 176] intentionally omitted <==**

The bleed air taken from later stages is much more expensive from an energy POV, therefore typically we try to use lower pressure bleed. 

## **Compressor characteristics** 

For any gas compressor we can express the dependence of the total pressure at the compressor outlet p02 and adiabatic efficiency on the other important variables: 

**==> picture [163 x 17] intentionally omitted <==**

90 

The relevant gas properties are specified by the kinematic viscosity ν, the density ρ, the specific heat ratio k, and the gas constant R. Design is the complete specification of the geometric shape of the machine and D is the characteristic size, while a is the speed of sound. ∆h is the enthalpy variation across the compressor and P is the output of the compressor. 

Dimensional analysis can be used to simplify the representation of these relations. This is a method which combines the variables involved to form a smaller and more manageable group of dimensionless variables. 

The first step is to check that all the variables on the right-hand side of the relation are actually independent. The second step is to reduce the relation to one in which each dimensionless dependent variable is expressed as a function of a lower number of independent dimensionless variables. By following these steps we get: 

**==> picture [211 x 69] intentionally omitted <==**

Radius and diameter can all be taken out this relations since they are constant. From this relation we can see that the pressure ratio and temperature ratio (directly related to the efficiency of the compressor) in the compressor can be expressed as a function of the non-dimensional mass flow rate, the non-dimensional rotation and the flow properties (Re and heat capacity ratios). Assuming constant flow properties we get: 

**==> picture [141 x 52] intentionally omitted <==**

We can represent this relation in a diagram called the **compressor map** . This “map” represents the performance of the compressor. 

**==> picture [257 x 193] intentionally omitted <==**

The compressor characteristic shows the most important quantities that influence the performance of the compressor. These quantities are grouped in dimensionless parameter groups: 

**==> picture [209 x 111] intentionally omitted <==**

91 

The design point is typically close to the maximum efficiency region. By changing the rpms of the compressor, the operating point shifts along the blue line beneath called **equilibrium line** . This design operating line needs to be distant enough from the surge line. This distance is called the **surge margin** . 

**==> picture [313 x 232] intentionally omitted <==**

## **Operational limits** 

## **Surge** 

To explain “surge”, we assume a compressor running at a fixed speed having an adjustable valve at the exit channel. By adjusting the position of the valve, the mass flow and the pressure ratio over the compressor can be controlled. The pressure ratiowhats can be plotted as function of the mass flow rate by slowly adjusting the valve positions. By opening the valve we get a curve similar to the “curve A”. When the valve is opened slowly, the pressure ratio will increase at first since the stator vanes’ contribution to the pressure ratio will increase and the inflow conditions to the blades will be better. This will continue until an optimum is reached (at operating point 2), after which the pressure ratio will decrease because the efficiency decreases as a result of an increasingly more negative angle of incidence on the blades. 

**==> picture [230 x 164] intentionally omitted <==**

The curve enclosed by points 2 and 3 is considered to represent stable operation of the compressor in contrast to the “unstable” curve up to point 1. The curve before point 1 is unstable because a disturbance can cause the mass flow to decrease, which would results in a pressure drop at the compressor outlet. This decrease in pressure will result in an adverse pressure gradient at the compressor outlet since the pressure in the next component (the combustion chamber) does not decrease immediately. This adverse pressure gradient slows down the flow and results in a further 

92 

decrease in mass flow rate. This continues until global flow reversal occurs in the compressor. In the mean time, since the compressor is not working anymore, the pressure downstream of it also drops, facilitating the compressor to pick up again, repeating the whole cycle. This whole cycle creates high frequency oscillations and it’s called **compressor surge** . From the figure below the “operating points” of a compressor in surge follow the red cycle, going from **mild surge** (no flow reversal) to **deep surge** (global flow reversal) again and again. The difference with the stall phenomenon is that the stall is a local disturbance of airflow with local flow reversal, while surge is characterised by a flow reversal of the entire compressor. However, the two are related as typically surge is preceded by stall. The position of the surge line is highly dependent on the dynamic character of the surge phenomenon and is not only compressor dependent but also dependents on the whole engine system. The surge line defines a hard lower limit for compressor operations that cannot be crossed. 

**==> picture [252 x 201] intentionally omitted <==**

We can also see that at lower rotational speeds the operating margin of the compressor reduces quite drastically and surge becomes a bigger risk. 

Surge in compressors has to be avoided because it causes periodically oscillating forces of high magnitude, which can set individual blades into vibration. The vibration of the blades will ultimately result in fracture (fatigue). Since the flow is reverted continuously during surge, hot compressor air at the end comes back to the first stages to get compressed again, causing the compressor to warm up. The warming up also contributes to the fracture in the form of thermal fatigue. 

Some ways to avoid surge are: 

- **Variable bleed valves** : bleed air from the compressors reduces the flow resistance downstream and increases the airflow in front of the blow-off. 

- **Variable stator vanes** : The front stages are fitted with variable stator vanes to provide optimal angles of airflow incidence for low spool speeds. 

- **Multi-spool compressor** : splitting up the compressor in different parts, with different rotating speeds allows to control the rotational speed of the front and back stages. 

## **Stall** 

Airflow separation occurs when the angle of attack becomes too large for the airflow to follow the blade profile. This referred to as **stall** . 

When a disturbance in the flow is observed stall can occur. In the figure below, for example, flow breakdown in channel B results in deflection of the inflow incidence at channel A and C. Channel A receives the fluid at an increased angle of incidence (positive direction) , while channel C receives the fluid at a reduced angle of incidence (negative direction). This will result in flow breakdown channel A, therefore the flow to channel B will now be  more favourable. It appears that the  blockage has moved opposite to the direction of rotation. This phenomenon is called  “ **rotating stall** ”. 

93 

**==> picture [245 x 147] intentionally omitted <==**

Rotating stall usually appears at the blade tips since the speeds are at maximum. In case the flow worsens, the stall areas will expand to grow to the length of the blade, which is called “full span stall”. This can then devolve into full on compressor surge. 

**==> picture [226 x 140] intentionally omitted <==**

Stall typically occurs at low rotor speeds because in these conditions we have the following velocity triangles: 

**==> picture [308 x 120] intentionally omitted <==**

When the rotor velocity is low, the pressure ratio is lower than what it typically is, which means that the density of the fluid at the final stages is much lower than usual. For the same mass flow rate, this means that the last stages see an axial velocity which is much higher than normal. This reduces the angle of attack that the blades of the last stages see, which can cause negative stall. The opposite occurs in the first few stages (same density, therefore similar axial velocity with a much lower rotational speed), leading to positive stall. 

The solution for these issues are the same as the ones for the surge. 

Note: one important element that determines the inflow conditions for the compressors is the shape of the **spinner** of the fan and the amount of distortion present at the fan face. We see that typically the spinner has a conical or elliptical shape, since these shapes help reduce the amount of distortion. Also we see that they heave very similar performances in clean flow. 

94 

**==> picture [242 x 189] intentionally omitted <==**

**Lecture 11** 

## **Axial turbine** 

The axial turbine has a vary similar architecture to the axial compressor. In this case however, it is the fluid that does work to move the turbine blades. This work is then used to operate the compressor. A typical turbine stage is made up of: 

- **Stator** : fluid expands and accelerates, so pressure decreases while velocity increases. Instead of the compressor stators, which act as diffusers, turbine stators act as nozzles. This is why they are sometimes called **Nozzle Guide Vanes** NGVs. The converging channels within the stator are accelerating the flow, usually to sonic and in some cases, supersonic velocities. This occurs because the flow in the turbine is already in the transonic regime and, after the expansion, it can reach sonic conditions, leading to the possible formation of shocks. 

**==> picture [281 x 190] intentionally omitted <==**

- **Rotors** : the fluid moves the turbine blades, work is extracted so velocity, total temperature and total pressure decrease. 

The flow inside the turbine is generally less complex than the flow in a compressor, since in this case the flow is expanding, so a favourable pressure gradient is present. This pushes the air through the turbine, unlike the adverse pressure gradient that is present in the compressor stages. For this reason turbines do not suffer from surge as compressors do. 

The velocity triangles of a turbine stage are as follows: 

95 

**==> picture [335 x 146] intentionally omitted <==**

[Note: the second velocity triangle in this figure is wrong since they should be the velocities relative to the stator, so either C1 and W1, or C3 and W3] 

We see that another difference with the compressor is that in this case the tangential velocity is much lower than the relative velocity, while in the compressor it’s the opposite. 

Also since in this case the rotor is being “pushed” by the air and not the other way around, the relative velocity it sees is much lower. This is the main reason why the turbine blades are much blunter that the compressor blades, since stall is less of an issue. Also the airfoils can be thicker, allowing for the cooling channels that bring the compressor bleed air to form a thermal protective film around the turbine blades. 

Just like for the compressor, since we typically want repeating stages, the exiting velocity of one stage has to be the same as the entering velocity. 

Finally we have to remember that the axial component of the absolute velocity is constant throughout and this is ensured by having an increasing cross section. 

**==> picture [297 x 138] intentionally omitted <==**

The variation of total pressure, static pressure and absolute velocity we can see that: 

**==> picture [257 x 205] intentionally omitted <==**

The total pressure decreases slightly in the stator due to losses and then decrease in the rotor due to the extraction of work, the static pressure decreases overall (the amount by which it decreases in each 

96 

section is determined by the degree of reaction) and the velocity increases in the stator and decreases in the rotor. 

Let’s now observe the T-S diagram of a turbine stage: 

**==> picture [166 x 169] intentionally omitted <==**

[Note: this figure represents a turbine with a degree of reaction equal to 1 since the static enthalpy does not change in the rotor stage] 

Due to the losses the total pressure from P01 to P02 decreases a bit. Due to the expansion, the static pressure from 1 to 2 decreases. This decrease is equal to the increase in kinetic energy of the gasses between stage 1 and 2. Then in the rotor the static pressure changes slightly depending on the degree of reaction, while the total pressure decreases drastically due to the extraction of work. The kinetic energy of the gasses at station 3 is the same as the kinetic energy of the gasses at station 1 since absolute velocity has to be the same. 

The power extracted can be evaluated from the Euler equation: 

**==> picture [212 x 32] intentionally omitted <==**

To extract/impart work to a fluid a change in momentum has to occur. For axial compressor and axial turbines, the change in total enthalpy is entirely due to change in tangential flow speed, while for radial compressors or radial turbines the change in total enthalpy is due to a change is radius (directly related to the centrifugal effect). 

Let’s analyse the turbine stage more closely: 

**==> picture [259 x 141] intentionally omitted <==**

Other important turbine parameters: 

97 

**==> picture [247 x 144] intentionally omitted <==**

The first relation can be simplified only because the total temperature does not change in the stator. The total temperature drop can then be used, together with the isentropic efficiency term to determine the pressure ratio per turbine stage. 

To study turbine performance three important parameters need to be defined: 

- **Flow coefficient** : ratio between the axial velocity of the flow and its tangential velocity. In a way it’s a measure of the amount of flow that can pass through the turbine stage. 

**==> picture [37 x 30] intentionally omitted <==**

- **Loading factor** : ratio between the enthalpy drop for a stage and the square of the tangential velocity. It defines the “skew” of the velocity triangles, since the more skewed they are (the higher the deflection of the flow is), then the higher the specific work that is being extracted per stage is. 

**==> picture [145 x 142] intentionally omitted <==**

- **Degree of reaction** : same definition as for the compressor. For an axial turbine a relation can be derived to evaluate it from the velocity triangles: 

**==> picture [230 x 34] intentionally omitted <==**

Typical aero-engine turbines have a degree of reaction of around 0.5. 

Just like for the compressor, the turbine performance can be evaluated from dimensionless parameters. The same dimensionless parameters used for the compressor map are used for the turbine. The **turbine map** is: 

98 

**==> picture [277 x 251] intentionally omitted <==**

The performance characteristics of a turbine are determined by the choking of the stator vanes. As the pressure ratio increases, the corrected mass flow rate increases until the turbine becomes choked and the mass flow rate plateaus. As the rotation of the turbine increases, more work is being extracted by the rotors (the decrease in velocity in the rotor is higher) and a higher pressure ratio is needed for the mass flow to become choked.
