Since the turbine is operating in a favourable pressure gradient we also see that its efficiency is not as dependent on the operating conditions as the efficiency of the compressor. This is also because once the flow becomes choked the incidence angles basically do not change. 

Since the turbine and the compressor are connected to each other and have drastically different performance characteristics, a compromise has to take place to determine the operational point, while keeping in mind that the rotational speed and the corrected mass flow rate are the same for both. Also the power and torque balance has to be taken into account. These three constraints determine the operating point of the turbomachinery. 

As we increase the number of stages in a turbine increases we see that the corrected mass flow rate (also called “ **swallowing capacity** ”) tends to decrease, since it is determined by the stage that can handle the lowest mass flow rate before choking. 

**==> picture [208 x 200] intentionally omitted <==**

99 

This is not really relevant for the high pressure turbine, since it typically has 1-2 stages, however it is relevant for the low pressure turbine since it usually is a multistage turbine. 

One way to determine the performance of turbines is to look at the **Smith chart** of the turbine, which represents the stage efficiency as a function of loading factor and flow coefficient: 

**==> picture [337 x 201] intentionally omitted <==**

A lower RPM increases the stage loading and the flow coefficient, while a higher RPM reduces them (for a given enthalpy jump and axial velocity). For a high pressure turbine the RPM is very high so it has a high efficiency regardless. However, the low pressure turbine typically has to rotate at a lower speed since it is also connected to the fan, which cannot have high rotational speed due to tip shocks. From the chart we can observe that a high loading factor corresponds to the flow being “turned” a lot (high skew of the velocity triangles), therefore extracting a lot of work per each stage, while also introducing losses. On the other hand a high flow factor corresponds to high passage area, since it is a measure of the swallowing capacity of the stage. 

High stage loading means that the number of stages needed can be reduced, thus reducing the total weight of the engine. However this does come at the disadvantage of lower turbine efficiency. In aeroengines, since the weight is one of the most critical design parameters, blades that turn the flow a lot are typically used to have higher loading factors at the cost of lower efficiency. 

The reason why the GTF is such an efficient engine is that the tangential velocity of the low pressure turbine can be much higher, since it is basically decoupled from the fan rotational speed. This allows it to have much higher turbine efficiencies even with high work extraction per stage. 

## **Turbine cooling** 

The maximum temperature that is allowed inside the engine is determined by the high pressure turbine blades, that receive the hot gasses from the combustion chamber at the TIT. Since we want to have the high temperature ratio to increase the thermodynamic efficiency, the turbine blades are built to withstand incredibly high temperatures. From the chart below we actually see that the TIT is typically much higher than the melting point of the material out of which the turbine blades are made out of. 

100 

**==> picture [300 x 199] intentionally omitted <==**

Advancements in material technology have made possible the manufacturing of turbine blades that have a **single crystal structure** without any grain boundaries. This eliminates any imperfections in the molecular structure of the material and greatly decreases the effects of high temperature **creep** which can lead to failure at high temperatures, especially when coupled with high stresses (centrifugal forces). 

The main reason for the discrepancy between the melting point of the material and the operating temperature is cooling, which is achieved by supplying compressor bleed air to the turbine blades. This air is released from tiny holes on the surface of the turbine blades and forms a protective thermal film which dissipates heat very efficiently. 

**==> picture [243 x 172] intentionally omitted <==**

One element present in some turbine blades is the **shroud** , which interlocks with the shroud of neighbouring turbine blades, forming a platform which reduces tip losses. It is not always used since it does increase the centrifugal force acting on the blades (worse mass distribution), effectively reducing their lifespan. 

The most critical region of the blade that needs to be cooled is the leading edge, since that is where the stagnation point of the incoming flow is and where the temperature reaches the maximum. To cool this part of the blade **impingement cooling** is used, where a channel dedicated to the leading edge blows air on the back of the it to promote heat dissipation. These jets create a very thin boundary layer on the surface and achieve extremely high local heat transfer coefficients. 

Note: the channels inside the blades that transport bleed air have all **turbulence promoters** to create turbulence and enhance heat transfer away from the walls. 

101 

**==> picture [215 x 204] intentionally omitted <==**

Jet impingement is also used in the **active clearance control system** , which is made up of small ducts in which compressor bleed air is run through. These ducts blow air on the casing of the turbine blades and can either expand the casing, allowing for more tip clearance when the engine is running at cruise (high temperatures and high centrifugal forces) or contract the casing, allowing for less tip clearance when the engine is running idle for example. 

**==> picture [129 x 127] intentionally omitted <==**

## **Lecture 12** 

The performance of a gas turbine aero engine is determined by: 

- Components characteristics: performance is determined by all the components we have seen so far as well as their limits (e.g. stall, temperature, ...) 

- Engine characteristics: the operating conditions obviously determine the engine’s performance 

- Ambient conditions 

The range of engine operating conditions is determined by superimposing the operational envelopes of all the components that make up the engine. The FADEC ( **Full Authority Digital Engine Control** keeps the engine within its operational limits. It can do this by controlling different variables and systems like: the fuel flow, variable stator vanes, variable bleed valves, active clearance control, nozzle geometry, after burner fuel flow, thrust vectoring, ... 

Let’s start with the compressor. As previously seen, the compressor’s characteristics are represented by the compressor map. 

102 

**==> picture [197 x 139] intentionally omitted <==**

The operating conditions of the compressor are typically represented along an equilibrium line. However, this is an ideal representation, which is valid only if the compressor is changing speed very slowly without strong accelerations (in other words if we do not take into account system dynamics). The operating conditions of a turbine is determined from the turbine map. 

**==> picture [193 x 177] intentionally omitted <==**

The turbine characteristic is less complex than that of the compressor since the turbine becomes choked after a certain pressure ratio, independently of the speed. During normal operations, the turbine is almost always choked. Moreover, the efficiency of the turbine does not change much once it becomes choked. 

Other components such as the ducts and inlets have much simpler characteristics. In this type of component, the only thing that matters is the pressure loss, which is generally a function of the flow velocity. For all components heat loss effects are also taken into account through sophisticated models to evaluate their characteristic performance. 

By superimposing all of the component characteristics we can obtain the characteristic of the whole gas turbine. 

**==> picture [179 x 129] intentionally omitted <==**

From this we can check the required temperature ratio for the gas generator to be self-sustaining, given an operational point of the compressor (I think). 

103 

The characteristic performance of a component can be adapted to the operating condition by changing the geometry of the component. For example, in a compressor it is possible to avoid the surge line when changing the operational point by implementing variable stator vanes (adapt the angle of attack of the stators, avoiding stall and surge) and variable bleed valves, which are typically positioned between the booster and the HPC (opening these valves reduces the back pressure and allows for an increase in mass flow rate, moving away from the surge line). The IGV can also be variable to improve the inlet condition of the flow entering the booster. 

Although stall is not very common in turbines (since there is a favourable pressure gradient) , the same thing can be applied to turbine through variable stator vanes. This has been tried in the past, leading to improved SFC but has not been implemented widely. 

Let’s now see how the performance of the whole engine (thrust and fuel consumption) changes with different component operating conditions. Regarding the compressor, an increase in speed directly correlates to an increase in thrust. 

**==> picture [185 x 146] intentionally omitted <==**

This is due to the fact that an increase in spool speed is directly correlated to an increase in pressure ratio and therefore energy available (i.e. thrust). For the TSFC we also see improvements as the spool speed increases at first, however after a certain threshold the trend changes. 

**==> picture [186 x 142] intentionally omitted <==**

The cruise spool speed is determined by this graph and is set at around 80-90%. With respect to Mach number and altitude: 

**==> picture [235 x 172] intentionally omitted <==**

104 

As we can see, when the altitude increases the thrust produced also reduces w.r.t. the design thrust. This is mainly due to the reduction in air density and therefore mass flow rate. The trend with increasing Mach number is the same. This is instead due to the increase in ram drag; from the thrust equation we know that the thrust is directly proportional to the difference in jet velocity and flight velocity. As the flight velocity increases it becomes harder and harder to provide the same velocity increase to the exhaust gasses. For the TSFC: 

**==> picture [221 x 175] intentionally omitted <==**

The behaviour of the thrust specific fuel consumption is very different from the thrust. The TSFC increases with Mach number always since it becomes harder to provide the same exhaust velocity increase w.r.t. the flight speed at higher Mach. 

However, it decreases with altitude until the tropopause is reached (after which the external temperature stays roughly constant). External temperature decreases, which corresponds to an increase in the temperature ratio between the turbine inlet temperature and the ambient temperature. From a thermodynamic point of view this is very beneficial for the efficiency, which leads to a decrease of TSFC. This is not the case for lower Mach numbers, where the decrease in outside temperature (higher thermodynamic efficiency) is outweighed by the decrease in pressure (lower thrust). Lower pressure and dynamic compression means that more work has to be done by the compressor to achieve the same thrust, therefore the TSFC actually increases with higher altitude (up until M~0.3). 

In summary: 

**==> picture [256 x 144] intentionally omitted <==**

To evaluate engine performance w.r.t. operating conditions we can define the **thrust lapse rate** . This parameter quantifies the reduction in thrust of an engine when compared to its design value at sea level and at M = 0 (uninstalled thrust). It can be defined w.r.t. Mach number or altitude: 

105 

**==> picture [205 x 150] intentionally omitted <==**

For a turbofan the thrust reduces with Mach number, while for a turbojet it first reduces and then increases again (after a certain point all the compression needed comes from ram compression). Let’s now consider the characteristics of the whole engine for a 2 shaft power turbine: 

**==> picture [209 x 174] intentionally omitted <==**

Where NPT is the speed of the power turbine that drives the load (the propeller) and NGG is the speed of the gas generator (other turbine and compressor). The operating point is determined by the rotating speed of the power turbine and the required power to drive the load PW. We can see that at low speeds for the power turbine the specific fuel consumption is quite high and it gets much better at higher NPT values. Same goes for the NGG. 

Another advantage of having a two shaft turboshaft is that it’s **torque characteristic** is much better than a one shaft turboshaft, especially at lower shaft speeds. 

**==> picture [227 x 151] intentionally omitted <==**

The gas turbine shaft can generate power (or torque) when it’s running at high speeds to drive the loaded shaft even if the power turbine is not running. This is the great advantage of two spool turboshafts. For one spool turboshafts starting up the power turbine takes a great amount of torque, which the turboshaft struggles to generate at low speeds. 

106 

**==> picture [227 x 140] intentionally omitted <==**

**Transient operations** 

Although all the ideal operational conditions lie on the engine equilibrium line (steady-state), in reality the transition between one operating state and another does not occur slowly enough so that all states in between are steady states. The real operating line moves closer/away from the surge line, depending on the change of speed. 

Each spool is governed by a rotational balance, where the change in rotational speed over time is proportional to the difference in power extracted by the turbine and the power consumed by the compressor. 

To increase the thrust generated by the engine, the fuel flow rate is increased, the TIT increases and the turbine speed increases. This means that the compressor starts working at a higher speed, while the corrected mass flow rate does not immediately increase and lags behind. 

This means that during the transient, the operating point of the compressor moves up (higher pressure ratio due to higher rotational speed), while having the same mass flow rate. This is the **acceleration line** , which occurs when there is **overfueling** (more fuel flow than needed to maintain the current steady state): 

**==> picture [233 x 147] intentionally omitted <==**

The exact opposite occurs when the thrust needs to be reduced. In this case the mass flow rate in the compressor lags behind the reduction in pressure ratio due to the reduction of spool speed. This is the **deceleration line** , which occurs when there is **underfueling** (less fuel flow is provided than needed to maintain the current steady state). 

**==> picture [223 x 156] intentionally omitted <==**

107 

## **Aero engine limitations** 

In the chart below the main aero engine operational limitations are represented. The ideal operational conditions all lie on the engine equilibrium line between the take off condition (maximum mass flow rate for max thrust) and idle (minimum mass flow rate). 

**==> picture [284 x 171] intentionally omitted <==**

These limits are taken into account by the FADEC, especially during transient operations. The increase of fuel flow needs to be limited to avoid surge or surpassing the maximum turbine inlet temperature. For lower mass flow rates surge is the limiting factor, since stall is more likely to occur due to higher incidence angles. For higher mass flow rates the turbine inlet temperature becomes the limiting factor since an increase in fuel flow corresponds to an increase in the final combustion temperature. 

The decrease of fuel flow needs to be limited to avoid lean blowout in the combustor. 

The idle limit is defined as the minimum speed required for the core gas generator to sustain itself (the minimum pressure ratio required for the thermodynamic cycle to be viable), while the max speed limited is imposed by the centrifugal forces acting on the disks of the turbines. 

Another way to determine the performance of an engine is to evaluate the **flat rating** . We say that an engine is flat rated if it delivers constant **rated thrust** (or shaft power) over a range of ambient temperatures and pressures, up to a specified “ **flat-rating temperature** ”, and becomes thermally limited only above that point. 

**==> picture [232 x 146] intentionally omitted <==**

[Note: the rated thrust is not the maximum thrust the engine can produce, it is the advertised value of thrust, which is lower than the max thrust for all temperatures lower than the flat rating. Over the flat rating this is not true anymore, so this serves as a warning for the operators] 

For lower temperatures the engine performance is deliberately limited by the FADEC. In this region it is the maximum pressure ratio that determines the maximum thrust that can be achieved. Still, this thrust could be obtained by increasing the TIT and the spool speeds. 

For higher temperatures the engine performance decays. In this region the engine becomes temperature limited, meaning that the maximum thrust is now limited by the maximum TIT (or the maximum spool speed), so the rated thrust has to decrease accordingly. 

108 

**==> picture [214 x 153] intentionally omitted <==**

The flat rating temperature and the rated thrust both decrease with altitude since the density of the incoming air is also reducing. 

**==> picture [260 x 161] intentionally omitted <==**

**Effect of design parameters on performance** 

For a turbofan the 4 main design parameters are the Bypass Ratio BPR, the Fan Pressure Ratio FPR, the Overall Pressure Ratio OPR and the Turbine Inlet Temperature TIT. Let’s now look at their impact on aero engine performance. 

**==> picture [252 x 182] intentionally omitted <==**

As we can see from the figure above, the fuel consumption decreases with increasing BPR, since the propulsive efficiency is increasing (to produce the same thrust the exhaust velocity of the gasses can be lower). Additionally, the dependence of the fuel consumption on the TIT decreases with increasing BPR. By keeping the OPR constant, the TSFC worsens with increasing TIT because the thermodynamic efficiency does not change much, while more fuel is being burned. 

109 

**==> picture [211 x 166] intentionally omitted <==**

If instead we keep the BPR fixed, we can observe the opposite trend: the TSFC decreases with increasing TIT. This discrepancy is caused by the fact that as TIT increases the OPR also increases if it is allowed to change, leading to a higher thermodynamic efficiency, that coupled with more net power available results in a decrease of the TSFC. This is the same reason why the TSFC decreases with increasing OPR. 

**==> picture [226 x 165] intentionally omitted <==**

For any given BPR there is an optimum FPR which minimizes the fuel consumption, and this optimum decreases with increasing BPR. This is due to the fact that with increasing BPR more and more thrust is generated by the bypass flow, whose velocity increase is mainly dictated by the FPR. If the BPR increases there will be more mass flow rate and therefore to produce the same amount of thrust, a lower pressure increase is required. 

There is a caveat however; when the FPR reduces a lot (typically to a value lower than 1.4) the bypass nozzle might not be choked in the cruise condition (since the pressure ratio in the nozzle is also decreasing), reducing the nozzle isentropic efficiency. 

To avoid stall, especially during take off, variable blades for the fan or variable geometry for the nozzle are required. 

If we also take into account the propulsive efficiency: 

**==> picture [225 x 151] intentionally omitted <==**

110 

The propulsive efficiency is determined by the BPR and the FPR (high BPR coupled with a lower FPR). From the graph above we can see that the TSFC can also be reduced by increasing the OPR or the TIT (at a given propulsive efficiency). 

Finally, we can look at the specific thrust: 

**==> picture [185 x 137] intentionally omitted <==**

The specific thrust increases with TIT since more net power can be extracted from the cycle. Also it decreases with Bypass Ratio at a given OPR. This is caused by the fact that as BPR increases, more and more of the thrust is generated by the fan and not by the increase in jet velocity. From the thrust equation and the definition of specific thrust we can see why this leads to a decrease of the latter. 

## **Lecture 13** 

## **Emissions** 

The aviation CO2 emissions have increased by ~2.5% YoY for the past two decades. Nowadays there are on average 40 mln flights per year and this number is projected to further increase in the next decades. Since emissions are directly proportional to the total number of km flown every year, this means that CO2 emissions will keep on growing. 

**==> picture [243 x 155] intentionally omitted <==**

The average fuel burn over the various phases of a typical mission can be seen in the figure below: 

**==> picture [377 x 153] intentionally omitted <==**

111 

The fuel flow for the take-off and cruise conditions is the same, however this is only because the taxi phase is also included in the take off phase, so on average the fuel burn rate is the same. The highest fuel burn in absolute value occurs in cruise, while the highest fuel flow is obtained during take-off since thrust is at a maximum. The lowest fuel flow is instead reached during landing. 

The major species that are produced in the combustion chamber are the following: 

**==> picture [443 x 177] intentionally omitted <==**

The main species are CO2 and water vapor since combustion occurs at very lean overall equivalence ratio, while everything else is a secondary emission. Although secondary species are produced at a much lower rate, this does not matter that their impact on the climate is lower than the main species produced. 

On top of these products **particulates** are produced, which can be classified into: 

**==> picture [302 x 151] intentionally omitted <==**

Note: ambient aerosols are particles that are already present in the air before the combustion in the engine. Non-volatile particulate matter nvPM is primarily made up of soot. 

Emissions certification for engines is done for an idealized landing and take-off at less than 3000 feet. The certification consist of four different tests at sea level, at four different thrust settings (7%, 30%, 85%, 100% of the thrust at sea level). These four thrust conditions are meant to simulate the four phases of the landing-take-off cycle: 

**==> picture [357 x 127] intentionally omitted <==**

112 

The regulated species are NOx, CO, unburnt hydrocarbons UHC, non-volatile particulate matter nvPM and smoke. 

As we have seen before in the combustion module, NOx and soot production is a function of flame temperature, while CO2 and UHC production is inversely proportional to it. 

To quantify the amount of emitted species the **emission index** is defined as: 

**==> picture [61 x 26] intentionally omitted <==**

Where x is the relevant chemical specie. 

The NOx emissions are actually made up of many different nitrogen oxides that are produced during combustion. To simplify the study of emissions however it is assumed that all nitrogen is converted into NO2. 

Since NO is also one of the main products, the molar rate of NO has to be converted into molar rate of NO2 (through their respective molar masses). This is then added to the actual molar rate of NO2 to evaluate the EI of NOx emissions. 

For CO2 the emission index depends strictly on the chemical composition of the fuel. Since it’s almost always a hydrocarbon, the number of moles of CO2 is equal to the number of moles of carbon present in the fuel. The issue however is that the chemical composition of hydrocarbon fuels is not constant, so the amount of CO2 produced does change. 

Specifically for NOx emissions, ICAO regulates them w.r.t. to the engine overall pressure ratio, since NOx emission increase with OPR. From the figure below we can see that over the years the certification limit has drastically reduced. 

**==> picture [337 x 215] intentionally omitted <==**

The EINOx increases YoY since the OPR of the engines is increasing, together with the absolute value of NOx emission since air traffic is increasing. However, the NOx emission per kilometre flown has been decreasing since engines have ben implementing newer combustion technologies that produce less NOx. 

In general improvements in efficiency correspond to a reduction in CO2 emissions (since they are directly proportional to fuel burned) and an increase in NOx emissions. 

An important thing to note is that single and twin aisle aircraft have different emission rates: 

113 

**==> picture [217 x 257] intentionally omitted <==**

We can see that, although single-aisle jets have a much bigger share of the total flights, their absolute emissions are very similar to the twin aisle jets for both CO2 and NOx. This is mainly caused by the much larger power requirement of the twin-aisle jets when compared to the single-aisle ones. 

To evaluate the impact of these emissions on the climate we have to consider the global energy balance. 

**==> picture [305 x 174] intentionally omitted <==**

Anthropogenic activities change this balance leading to some of the energy being retained in the atmosphere. This leads to a global temperature increase and climate change. This effect can be seen over the years in the figure below. 

**==> picture [287 x 139] intentionally omitted <==**

114 

The impact of emissions can be divided based on the part of the atmosphere in which they occur. At ground level the main focus is air quality control, so the most important emissions that need to be considered are NOx, particulate matters and noise emission. 

Then in the troposphere (cruise altitude) the focus shifts to greenhouse gasses, which trap long wave radiation inside of the atmosphere. 

**==> picture [316 x 157] intentionally omitted <==**

Air control is especially important at ground level since the chemical species emitted have very serious health implications. 

The impact of emissions on the atmosphere can be quantified through **radiative forcing** . This parameter is a measure of how a chemical specie changes Earth’s energy balance by altering the difference between incoming solar radiation and outgoing infrared radiation. If this parameter is zero there is no effect on the balance, while if it’s positive it means that the effect of said chemical specie is to “trap” more radiation in the atmosphere than it lets out (negative effect on climate change). The radiative forcing of the main products of aero engines can be seen below: 

**==> picture [343 x 282] intentionally omitted <==**

Although some chemical species can have a partial cooling effect (e.g. NOx reduces the concentration of methane, which has a cooling effect), all of the emission have an overall warming effect (e.g. NOx increases the ozone concentration, which trumps the positive effect of reducing methane concentrations). Each chemical specie contributes to climate change in different ways, as can be seen below: 

115 

**==> picture [413 x 152] intentionally omitted <==**

Note: NOx emissions change the concentration of different greenhouse gasses but they are not greenhouse gasses themselves. It increases the concentration of ozone (heating effect) and decrease the concentration of methane (cooling effect). Nevertheless, overall NOx emission have a heating effect. 

Aerosols are suspended particles in the atmosphere and have two main effects: they act as nuclei for condensation, helping the formation of contrails and scatter/absorb incoming radiation. The radiative forcing of aerosols is unsure but is typically regarded as negative overall (warming effect). 

Contrails, short for condensation trails, are vapour trail made up of ice crystals that are formed in the exhaust plume of the engine. The water concentration in the exhaust gasses is more than that in the atmosphere due to the water vapor formed as a product of combustion. This water vapor freezes at the low ambient temperature that corresponds to cruise altitudes and in the presence of nucleating particles such as soot. 

Contrails change the total cloudiness of the atmosphere, breaking the global energy balance. Contrail formation is governed by the **relative humidity** of the air, which is defined as the ratio between the partial pressure of water vapor and the equilibrium vapor pressure of water at the same temperature: 

**==> picture [181 x 34] intentionally omitted <==**

Where pH2O is the partial pressure of the water vapour and el(T) is the saturation pressure of water at temperature T. 

A mixture of water vapor and liquid water is saturated at a given temperature, if the exchange of water molecules between gas-phase and liquid phase is in equilibrium (meaning that the amount of water condensing and evaporating is equal). 

The relative humidity determines which transformation prevails: if it’s higher than one it means that the partial pressure of the vapor is higher than that of an equivalent system that is at equilibrium, therefore some vapor needs to liquify. Vice versa, if it’s lower than it means that the vapor pressure is lower than that of an equivalent system that is saturated at a given temperature, so some liquid water evaporates. 

**==> picture [452 x 119] intentionally omitted <==**

The same process can be repeated for the ice phase. A relative humidity can be defined w.r.t. the ice phase as: 

116 

**==> picture [181 x 41] intentionally omitted <==**

When the exhaust gasses and the cold outside air mix, the relative humidity changes and water vapour is transformed into ice crystals. Note that nucleating particles are important in this process; far fewer atmospheric particles can act as ice nuclei than as condensation nuclei (particles around which the transition process can occur). The relative humidity is temperature dependent: as temperature decreases the relative humidity increases, meaning that it becomes easier for ice crystals to form. 

**==> picture [195 x 189] intentionally omitted <==**

The process of contrail formation goes as following: 

- Engine core exhaust is hot (~700 K) and humid. 

- The ambient air during cruise altitudes is cold (~220K at 11km) and dry. 

- The exhaust mixes with the environment, which constantly reduces temperature, but increases relative humidity. 

- During this mixing, the air gets saturated with respect to liquid water. Droplets are formed. (RH>1) 

- Supercooled droplets freeze when temperature is < -38 ̊C. 

- Contrails form 

If  the atmosphere is humid then the ice crystals persist and contrails remain in the atmosphere for longer periods of time. However, if the atmosphere is dry then the ice droplets quickly turn back into water vapour and the contrails dissipate. 

The tendency to form contrails can be evaluated through the **Schmidt-Appleman criterion** . The criterion is derived using a mixing-line analysis in a temperature–humidity (or vapor pressure– temperature) plane. The mixing line is the line along which all the possible thermodynamic states of the two mixing streams lie (i.e. the exhaust and the outside air). 

**==> picture [373 x 151] intentionally omitted <==**

117 

In general, the higher the slope of the mixing line is, the wider the conditions for contrail formation are. The slope of the mixing line can be evaluated as: 

**==> picture [86 x 27] intentionally omitted <==**

Where pa is the atmospheric pressure, eps is a constant molar mass, eta is engine dependent and EI,H2O and Q are fuel dependent parameters. As we have already said, a steeper slope corresponds to a larger contrail formation probability. If the aircraft is more effective (i.e. higher 𝜂), the temperature of the exhaust gasses is actually lower and contrail formation is promoted. On the other hand if the higher heating value of the fuel Q is larger then the slope will be lower and the contrail formation probability will be lower. 

**==> picture [452 x 167] intentionally omitted <==**

In summary: 

- **Contrail formation** → affected by aircraft propulsion efficiency, fuel type and ambient condition (Schmidt Appleman Criterion applies) 

- **Contrail persistence** (temperature < -38 ̊C; relative humidity with respect to ice > 100%)→ affected by weather 

- **Contrail properties** (optical depth, etc.)→affected by particle emissions 

Non-CO2 emission are very different from CO2 emissions: 

**==> picture [351 x 143] intentionally omitted <==**

Note: the high confidence level for the CO2 emissions means that their impact is very well understood. 

The main mitigation measures to combat the effect of climate change are: 

118 

**==> picture [321 x 170] intentionally omitted <==**

When in comes to engine technologies: 

**==> picture [394 x 148] intentionally omitted <==**

In particular, for the combustion technologies lean burn technologies are able to greatly decrease the NOx emissions and particulate emissions by reducing the maximum temperature reached in the combustion chamber with minimal impact on engine performance. 

For alternative energy sources we have either drop-in solutions (e.g. SAF) or liquified hydrogen. SAF can be blended with traditional fuels at different levels with limits between 10% and 50% and can lead to a maximum reduction of 50% in climate change impact due to the non-CO2 emissions effect. 

When it comes to hydrogen, CO2 and nvPM emission can be completely eliminated, however water vapour production is greatly increased. More research however is required since the effect are still not very well known. 

To summarize, CO2 and H2O are greenhouse gasses since they both absorb outgoing longwave radiation. NOx emissions enhance ozone formation and methane depletion, both of which are greenhouse gasses. However, based on the current level of understanding, the overall effect is warming. Contrail formation depends on the efficiency of the engine, fuel type and ambient conditions. There is no single solution which eliminates all of these emissions. It’s always a trade-off between CO2 and NOx formation. 

119 

**==> picture [388 x 573] intentionally omitted <==**

120

## AE 4238 

## **Enthalpy** 

There are three types of Enthalpy 

1. Sensible 

2. Latent 

3. Reaction 

## **Sensible < Latent < Reaction** 

## _**Enthalpy of formation**_ 

_The enthalpy of formation hf  at a given temperature is defined as the heat released from producing 1 mole of a substance from its elements at that temperature._ 

_By this definition, the enthalpy of formation is zero for the reference elements._ 

**==> picture [116 x 52] intentionally omitted <==**

_These elements are, for example, graphite (C), molecular hydrogen (H2), molecular oxygen (O2), molecular nitrogen (N2), and molecular chlorine (Cl2)._

## AE 4238 

## **Bond energy** 

**==> picture [439 x 286] intentionally omitted <==**

- Energy required to separate two atoms. 

- The bond energy is defined as the min. potential energy relative to when the atoms are separated at infinity 

- The minimum point indicates equilibrium 

**==> picture [116 x 52] intentionally omitted <==**

Winterbone and Turan “ Advanced Thermodynamics for Engineers”, 2015 

## AE 4238 

## **Hess Law** 

If a reaction (isobaric or isochoric) is carried out in stages, the algebraic sum of the heat evolved in separate stages is equal to the total amount of heat released when the reaction occurs directly. 

Which means that the heat liberated in a reaction is independent of the path of the reaction between the initial and the final states. 

**==> picture [116 x 52] intentionally omitted <==**

## AE 4238 

## **Some observations** 

- The enthalpies of reaction for many hydrocarbon fuels on mass basis is similar, between 40-50 MJ/kg 

- The stoichiometric air-fuel ratio for many hydrocarbons lie between 13:1 to 17:1 

- Some fuels have positive formation of enthalpies 

- All fuels have negative enthalpies of reaction 

- The enthalpies of reaction for alcohols is less than non-oxygenated fuels (as oxygen does not provide any energy of reaction) 

**==> picture [116 x 52] intentionally omitted <==**

- Commonly used fossil fuels are a mixture of several hydrocarbon compounds (discussed later)

## AE 4238 

## **Conventional combustor** 

## **1. Primary Zone** 

- Anchors Flame 

- Provides sufficient time, mixing, temperature for “complete” oxidation of fuel 

- – Equivalence ratio near f=0.8-1.0 

## **2. Intermediate (Secondary Zone)** 

- **Low altitude** operation (higher pressures in combustor) 

   - Recover dissociation losses (primarily CO → CO2) and Soot Oxidation 

   - Complete burning of anything left over from primary due to poor mixing 

- **High altitude** operation (lower pressures in combustor) 

   - Low pressure implies slower rate of reaction in primary zone 

- Serves basically as an extension of primary zone (increased tres) 

- – L/D ~ 0.7 

## **3. Dilution Zone** 

- Mixing  air to lower temperature to acceptable value for turbine 

- Tailor temperature profile (low at root and tip, high in middle) 

**==> picture [116 x 52] intentionally omitted <==**

- Uses about 20-40% of total ingested core mass flow 

- L/D ~ 1.5-1.8

## **Conventional combustor** 

## **1. Primary Zone** 

- Anchors Flame 

- Provides sufficient time, mixing, temperature for “complete” oxidation of fuel 

- – Equivalence ratio near f=0.8-1.0 

## **2. Intermediate (Secondary Zone)** 

- **Low altitude** operation (higher pressures in combustor) 

   - Recover dissociation losses (primarily CO → CO2) and Soot Oxidation 

   - Complete burning of anything left over from primary due to poor mixing 

- **High altitude** operation (lower pressures in combustor) 

   - Low pressure implies slower rate of reaction in primary zone 

- Serves basically as an extension of primary zone (increased tres) 

- – L/D ~ 0.7 

## **3. Dilution Zone** 

- Mixing  air to lower temperature to acceptable value for turbine 

- Tailor temperature profile (low at root and tip, high in middle) 

**==> picture [116 x 52] intentionally omitted <==**

- Uses about 20-40% of total ingested core mass flow 

- L/D ~ 1.5-1.8

## AE 4238 

- 

## **Characteristics of Aero Engine Combustor** 

   - Primarily turbulent combustion 

- External ignition (e.g. spark) is required to light off only, then combustion is selfsustained 

- Constant (nearly) pressure combustion 

- Pressure losses penalize cycle performance and have to be limited (  4%, during cruise & 7% during T-O) 

- Typically, low relative volume (intensity up to 150 MW/atm-m[3] ) and structural weight: 

- Fast volume and heat dynamics, low surface-to-volume ratios 

- Low residence time for low NOx (~5 ms) 

- No heat rejection, no work extraction from/in combustor: 

- Combustor is insulated, heat losses penalize cycle performance 

**==> picture [116 x 52] intentionally omitted <==**

## AE 4238 

## **Characteristics of Aero Engine Combustor** 

## **Fuels:** 

- Gaseous & liquid 

- Natural gas & products of gasification (power generation) 

- • Kerosene & synthesized liquid (aviation) 

**Elevated/high pressure and air inlet temperature:** 

- Aircraft propulsion: up to 40-60 bar & >900 K 

- Power generation: <30 bar & <750 K 

- Micro turbines: 3.0-4.5 bar & up to 1050 K (due to recuperation) 

## **Combustor “types”:** 

- Conventional 

- 

- 

- 

- RQL, Lean Burn, staged combustors, etc 

- Alternative technologies: Lean Direct Injection, Flameless Combustion, etc Use of catalysts & inhibitors

## Combustion type 

## Diffusion burning: 

**==> picture [157 x 286] intentionally omitted <==**

- burning at ER ~ 1 

- • flashback free 

- very stable 

- reliable ignition 

- broad operat. envelope 

- high NOx 

- easy to control CO 

- high soot 

## Premix burning: 

- burning at selected ER 

- prone to flashback 

- prone to instabilities 

- unreliable ignition 

- narrow operat. envelope 

- low NOx 

- difficult to control CO 

- low soot

AE 4238 

## Comparison: Aero & Industrial 

**==> picture [116 x 52] intentionally omitted <==**

|**Criteria**|**Industrial**|**Aero**|
|---|---|---|
|Weight & Size|Not critical|Critical|
|Operating conditions|Sea level|Wider than the flight<br>envelope|
|No. Of cycles|30,000-50,000|6000-8000|
|Equivalent OperatingHours(EOH)|100,000-150,000|30,000-40,000|
|Emission regulations|Verystringent|Stringent|
|Criticalpoint in design|Lean blowout|Relight|
|Fuel flexibility|Important|Not critical|
|Fuel type|MostlyNaturalgas|Kerosene|
|Active air distribution|Possible|Notpossible|
|Residence time|10-30 ms|5 ms|
|Combustor Type|MostlyCan Type|MostlyAnnular type|
|Combustionphilisophy|MostlyLean, reheat|RQL/TAPS/Staged|

## Jet engine intake 

- Essentially a fluid flow duct whose task is to process airflow in a way that 

- ensures the engine functions properly 

- Provide adequate amount of uniform flow 

- Cruising: conversion of Ekin into Epot (pressure) 

- Ram compression / “Ram recovery” 

- Supersonic vs. subsonic intake 

- Subsonic inlets are dominated by the boundary layer behavior. 

- Supersonic inlets are dominated by the shock structure 

- From 1D compressible aerodynamics 

- dA/A = (M[2] -1)*(dV/V) 

- “Bellmouth” intake (test bed/stationary)

## Engineering Practice 

- is easier to measure 

- ram 

- and 

- is  ram can be used interchangeable till M<0.8 

- For supersonic inlets “stagnation pressure ratio” or “pressure recovery factor” or “Ram Recovery Factor” is used = P /P 0,2 0,0 

- RR (Ram Recovery) factor ~ 0.6 - 0.97 

- Sometimes Empirical relations are used for supersonic inlets 

   - P02/P00 = 1.0 – 0.075 (M-1)[1.35]

## Operation 

## **Subcritical:** 

- Mach number below the design value. 

- The normal shock occurs ahead of the cowl lip. 

- High external drag due to spillage. 

## **Critical:** 

- Design point operation. 

- The normal shock is located exactly at the cowl lip. 

## **Supercritical:** 

- Normal shock occurs in a region of higher Mach number 

- Mass flow is the same as the critical mode.

## Important Points 

▪ This is a 4 ECTS course. 

- The primary mode of teaching would be flipped classroom. 

- _**Flipped classroom is not an online course**_ 

- Most of the Lectures have been recorded and available on BrightSpace. 

- Students should go through the online-lecture before coming to the classroom. 

- During the lecture we will discuss your doubts and questions. 

- Lectures will follow the schedule mentioned on the timetable 

- Please ask questions to the instructors. We encourage interaction! 

- The TA’s will have an open hour to answer your questions and help you in your studies. 

- This is an MSc course, thus the course is shaped by you, we are just facilitators ☺ 

**==> picture [91 x 36] intentionally omitted <==**

38 

AE4238 

## Assessment 

- The grading shall be based on a final exam + Assignments. 

- Overall, the division of grade between the assignments and the final exam is 30-70. 

- However, you have to get a minimum of 5.5 grade points in the final exam to pass the course. So, if you do well in the assignments but fail to get 5.5 grade points in the final exam, it will still be considered as FAIL. 

- There will be around 2 assignments in total. 

- You might be randomly called by the instructor for a discussion w.r.t. the assignments. 

- Please note that plagiarism is taken seriously in TU, so avoid copying or cheating in any way
